WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Throne.—There may have been, and indeed was a time, when people in this country asked themselves whet was the use of a King and Royal Family,and when the answer which first suggested itself was, that their main use was to deliver us from something worse. But it is safe to say that since Queen Victoria came to the throne, it has been possible to suggest much more adequate reasons for their existenceamd if thequestion were asked to-day, few persons would experience any difficulty in supplying a satisfactory answer. The King more than justifies his office by his efforts to maintain the peace of Europe, which may one day preserve us honourably from a greet and terrible war, and further by the recognition—often worth more than a large sum of money—which he extends to so many good and meritorious causes. In this latter respect, his 'Majesty 3ei nobly seconded by the Queen, who, with tee Empress Marie of Russia, has been paying a succession of visits to the London hospitals and institutions. The members, of the Royal Family are net accustomed to consider their own convenience, when the opportunity offers, of assisting some enterprise that is calculated to promote the general welfare. • Royal Visit to the West.—The Prince and. Princess of Wales, who have rendered great service to the Empire •by their visits to our Colonies and independencies abroad, and have by their presence promoted the success of many important functions at home, are announced to make in June a motor tour of Devon and Cornwall, where they will certainly receive a most cordial wel- come. In the course of their thur, they will visit the Royal Cornwall Agricultural Show, at St.Colomb, and in so doing will not, only manifest their continued interest in an industry which the King and Prince of Wales have done much to foster, but will, no doubt, contribute very materially to the success, financially and otherwise, of a most useful enterprise. On Ghosts.—Mr. Elliot O'Donnell's lecture on the subject of ghosts should he of interest to many who declare that they have seen and believe in such visita- tions. As it is generally impossible to prove a negative, one can only accept their statements, but if spirits do at any time revisit the world; it is only reasonable to suppose that they even more than our- selvot, are under some control, and it is inconceivable that they would be permitted to appear solely for the purpose of frightening peop'e who have done them no harm. If there are these unlovely ghosts about, as Mr. Elliot O'Donnell, and many other people believe, belonging to a remote generation, then it would be interesting to know %hat is their opinion of some of our modern developments. Dreams.—Shakespeare hes much to say concerning • dreams, but none can tell us what they are or whence they come. Some time ago, a teacher of phychology soled a number of his women etudents to remember end record as far as possible, their dreams during a period of six nights. His object was to i scertain the causes and experiences which led to the dreams, whether they were recent or remote, but nothing very &finite seems to have been ascertained. There are characteristic dreams, which come to all habitual dreamers, such as picking up money, floating in the air, falling over a precipice, and the inability to run or escape from some immediate danger. If one is particularly afraid of firearms, spiders motor-cars or pews, these things will often find a place in our dreams, which have a %my of finding out our weaknese.es. But beyond the fact that these elusive wanderings of the unconscious brain are sometimes pleasant; and often very unpleasant, we know very little about dreams, which Shakespeare says, are "begot of nothing, but vain fantasy." In Memory of Sbakespeare.—Shakespeare was a parishioner of St. Saviour's Church—now Southwark Cathedral—on the South side of London Melee, for over fifteen yews; and fur the first time, the poet's con- nection with Southwark is to be recognised by a com- memorative service in the Cathedral on Shakespeare day—shakespeare's brother Edmund—an actor who resided at Bankside —was buried there. That was previous to the death of William Shakespeare on April 13, 1616, which euent many years afterwards was attributed by the Vicar of Stratford-en-Avon, to a merry meeting with Ben' Jenson, and Drayton. It was said that on this occasion Shakispeare drank too hard, and that the excess was primarily the cause of his death at the early age of fifty-two. Youthful Precocious Manners.—Other days, other manners. rills dictum finds an illustration in the t story of the bos s who, when told by the tobacconist that lie cOnld not be served with cigarettes owing to his being apparently under 16. replied "Oh, that's all right, I'll send father for them." The theory of education which taught children that they were not the most important ‘people in the world, seems to have disappeared long ago. Nowadays they are treated as such, and told as much. One often meets with them in railway travelling and not unfrequently fond parents have a way of conducting a lively conversation with a "young hopeful" in order to show other occupants of the carriage what a clever little boy be is. "Shots lag off" in this way is offensiue to people who are old-fashioned enough to think that little boys should be sem and not beard.

---Tl:e Early Morning Cup of Tea.—The medical journals are .ouce more up in arms against the early morning cup of tea, which, they tell us, may be harmful, when taken on an empty stomach. That, no doubt 13 the £950 in some instances, and if there is any feeling of after discomfort, the cup of tea should be discarded for the glass of plain hot water. Many of course, take nothing, but it is a very healthful practice to drink half a pint of hot water or weak tea, half an hour before breakfast. The belief that tea and coffee are bad for the nerves is a very old one, and in the reign of George I. an enterprising London grocer, advertised a cordial to counteract these effects. But, if. these beverages are properly made, the experience of most i people s much the same as Voltai cos who, when told that coffee was a poison, after he had drunk it con- stantly, for seventy years, remarked that it was at any rate" a slow one." American Fashions.—ThBre is an influential movement in the United States which demands that the fashion shall be American, and not Parisian. There has been a meeting of ell the leading dress de.signws, with the object of throwing off the foreign yoke and the move- ment has the &Wye support of Mts. Taft, the wae of the new President. Paris, however, laughs at this attempt to boycott her decrees, and it will probably be found in America as it has often been found in other countries, that patriotism is not the strong point of the woman of fashion. Summer Muffs. Florists will welcome the new muff, which so many fashionable women are carrying—end will carry, it is said, throughout the summer—built of airy-fairy fabrics to match the nook scarf, and bearing a cluster of flowers, either real or imitation. Needless to say real blooms and foliage are regarded as preferahle, but when imitation floe era are chosen, a little natural foliage apd a bloom or two, are generally tucked in among them. What .with the revival of the shower bouquet, and real roses in the hair at night, cossage knots of real flowers more in favour than ever by day, and the craze for natural flowers atid foliage in hats, said now muffs, the seasons should proro a-good ens for florists genersliv.

Dyed Lace.—The gold and silver laces so much in vogue during the winter months have almost dis- appeared, and their place has been taken by the dyed varieties. Occasionally a dyed lace, upon a fabric it matches precisely looks well, but usually the notion is a mistaken one. There is invariably a want of refine- ment about dyed lace, however skilfully manipulated, and we can only hope that the modistes who are " booming " it will speedily see the error of their ways. MADGE.

WHAT IS TARIFF REFORM?

" Tariff Reform " is a proposed revolutionary change in our system of national taxation—a change that would affect, for better or for worse, every household in the kingdom. At present our Government imposes Tariff duties or taxes upon only a very small number of the com- modities imported into this countr3; from abroad. The chief commodities thus taxed are alcoholic drinks, tobacco, tea, coffee, sugar, and substances con- taining sugar. None of these commodities excepting alcoholic drinks is produced in this country ; and it is important to note that these drinks, when manufac- tured in this country, are taxed equally with those imported from abroad. • FREE TRADE v. TARIFF REFORM.

The merits of our present system by comparison with that of the Tariff Reformers are, firstly, that the whole of the money raised 11 these taxes goes into the National Exchequer; and, secondly, that the cost of collecting the taxes is comparatively small. Tariff Reformers, on the other hand, propose to put taxes on all commodities, whether food commodities or manufactured goods, that are brought into this country from abroad, subject to two exceptions or qualifications. Firstly, they say that no Tariff taxes are to be imposed upon raw materials for use in our manufactures. Secondly, that conunodities imported from our Colonies or other Dominions are either to be" admitted free or are to be taxed more lightly than those imported from foreign countries. Tariff Reformers argue that these proposals would have the following beneficial results : 1. Additional -revenue would be brought into the National Exchequer. 2. The importation of foreign manufactured goods into this country would be discouraged, thus pro- viding more employment for British labour. 3. The importation of foreign food commodities would be discouraged, thus stimulating the production of these commodities at home and in our Colonies. In , exchange for the benefit thus resulting to the Colonies, it is expected that they would give a " preference " to our manylacturel by imposing lower Tariff duties upon these than upon foreign manufactures. The minimum Tariff Taxes proposed by Tariff Reformers upon commodities imported into this country are 5 per cent. upon food, and 10 or 15 per cent. upon manufactured goods. But there is no unanimity or official support of the Tariff Reform League regarding percentages. Plainly, the most important of these proposed taxes are the taxes upon imported food. Tariff Reformers maintain that food taxes at the rate of 5 per cent. upon imported corn and meat, being only Is. on each sovereign's worth, would not make food any dearer to the consumer because the foreigri growers of the food commodities would sell them 5 per cent. more'cheaply for fear of losing the British market. .

===== 0002601_19090417_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNDAMENTAL ERROR.

Actual experience of food taxation, however, both in this country and abroad, has cqpclusively proved that these anticipations of the Tariff Reformers are entirely mistiken. One of the ablest Conservative Chancellors of the Exchequer, Sir Michael Hicks-Beach (now Lord St. Aldwyn), believed at one time that a tax of is. per 4801 b. on imported corn would not make flour any dearer to the consumer. Afterwards he frankly confessed that he found himself mistaken ; for, in fact, corn became is. dearer immediately the tat was imposed, and later, when the tax was taken off, corn immediately became is. cheaper. Again, in Germany and other countries which have had experience of food taxes, it has been found that food prices rose by at least the amount of the taxes, and sometimes more than that. For example, at the beginning of August, 1908, the price of wheat in Lon- don *as 31s. 14 for 48011). In Berlin at the same time wheat of the same quality was 435. Bd. for 4801 b. or us. 10d. dearer; and 1 ls. 10d. was the precise amount of the German tax. Two months afterwards, in the middle of October, the London price was 31s. 21, and the 'Berlin price 445., being 12s. 10d. dearer, or actually Is. more than the amount of the German tax. British agriculturists laugh at the notion that food taxation would not raise prices. The very purpose and intention of the taxation is to raise prices—otherwise, the Tariff Taxes would be no 'encouragement to the growing of British corn. _ -

It sho—uld be clearly understood that tho proposal of Tariff Reformers is to impose the 5 per cent. Tariff taxation on all kinds of imported food. Mr. - Joseph Chamberlain at first proposed that no Tariff duties should be put upon imported maize nor upon bacon, because British farmers need maize at its cheapest as food for their animals,and because imported bacon is an important food of the poorest working people, Mr. Chamberlain thus admitting, by implication, that duties do enhance prices. Now, however, practically all Tariff Reformers are agreed that the 5 per cent. Tariff must fall on all food commodities alike. EFFECT OF FOOD TAXES ABROAD.

Let us remember also that the food taxes impostd by foreign Governments on their imports of food com- modities were small at first. and were afterwards made much heavier, on account of the complaints of the agriculturist that the small taxes did not sufficiently compensate them for the increase in the cost of living caused to them by the 'Protection Tariff system. Foreign experience is a surer guide as to what we May expect than are the promises made by our Tariff Reformers at present, when they are striving their best to render their proposals attractive to the masses of the people. Even now, British farmers declare that the 5 per cent. tax on wheat Will not be nearly enough to enable them to grow wheat against foreign and Colonial competition. The earnings of the farmers, we must remember, will be reduced hr the increased cost of feeding their animals, consequent on the taxatiOn of imported feeding stuffs and agricultural machinery, and they will, therefore, clamour for compensation in other directions. PRODUCERS FIGHT CONSUMERS.

In fact, as we see from the example of other countries, the only Ultimate limit to the weight of the food taxation will be the result of the perpetual struggle between landowners and farmer on the one hand to force the taxes higher, and of the consnmers on the other hand to keep them down. As it is more difficult for consumers to combine than for producers, the result of the struggle can only be in favour of the latter.

In this serif.rs of specially written articles, we purpose answering the various. arguments used by advo- cates ef Tariff Reform.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

[-By Redcap.] ACCOMMODATION AND HOUSING.

Many people after the perusal of a poultry appliance .catalogue, ask. themselves : Why such a _ .

variety of houses? Are they necessary?

And will

Lot one class of house do as well as another?

It is My intention in this article to explain why such a variety is necessary to accord with the accom- modation at your disposal. If y.ou.get the "'Hen Fever " thoroughly, depend upon it you will keep poultry' somewhere. It is most interesting to relate that ninny leading poultry fanciers in their Young days, and even in_their later days, have kept their birds under -peculiar and adverse circumstances. I am irnpleasantly remindedpf serious trouble on one occasion through utilising a back bedroom as tem- porary roosting quarters for a trio of Bantams I had purchased. This was being done quite secretly, but my pets let the " eat out of the bag" by perching not on the place provided for them, but on a pot of marmalade which eventually gave way under, the bidden and came with a terrible thud on the room floor. Investigation by my wife soon ended in a' speedy removal, and they had to be put up for the night in a washing-tub. After such an experience I strongly recommend my readers always to fix up ample accommodation before purchasing stock. Whatever your accommodation mafbe, whether it is that you are .a farmer with unlimited aredor whether you are a suburban dweller with not much land, or if it is your lot that you reside in the thickly populated part of a town with only a back-yard as your poultry run, all are catered for as far as poultry house construction goes.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FARMER'S POULTRY HOUSE.

It very much depends vrhat-line of poultry farming he takes up. If it is mainly for egg .production, and he can place a meadow at the entire disposal of his stock, then I should recommend moveable poultry houses. This class of house is built both on wheels and on sledges with space underneath for the fowls to run under during inclement weather: I give prefer- ence to the sledge house, because a warmer shelter is obtained seeing that the sides and one end of the house are boarded right down to the ground. This is not so with the.house built on wheels. It cannot be, because sufficient clearance from the ground has to be allowed for easy navigation. Thus while you have a shelter from the rain you have not from rough winds, and the conse- quence is that the fowls, seek shelter in the sleeping compartment and this is very undesirable for the best results. If the shelter is both warm and dry, as it would be providing the sledge house were used, your fowls would be kept at exercise while sheltering, by scratching in the dry earth underneath the house and also having a necessary dust 'bath at the same time. By adopting this system the birds would- rarely visit the sleeping quarters except to lay, until the sun went down.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
PERMANENT POULTRY' RUNS,

Should the farmer decide that a certain position be used as a permanent run, then I should recommend a double run system. This could be best explained by diagram, but space will not allow so I will do my best to make it clear. The double run is obtained by dividing a piece of ground into two sections, each run being accessible to fhe fowls from the same house. This system is a good one, as one run is always resting and being sweetened by the weathei, while the other is occupied. The most suitable hous6' when this plan is adopted is what is known as the

SCRATCHING SHED and poultry house combined. These are built so as to provide a well-ventilated roosting house and a roomy scratching shed combined- under the one roof, and if these sheds are provided With a good deep layer of litter on the floor they keep your birds at exercise when not in the open run. This class of poultry house is the very best accommodation for poultry in a limited run. Nothing can be worse for your stock than if kept in a run where no shelter is available except the roosting place, for if the weather is bad they get tired of huddling together on the perches and mope about in the wet, all this tending 'to reduce vitality and encourage disease. It is absolutely indis- penseble for succesSful poultry farming that your fowls should hrrve at least one dry corner to scratch in when the weather is bad.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
SUBURBAN PITNS

If it were not that the suburban poultry run is limited in space; I should have recommended the scratching shed system here. - However, the class of house best suited for this accommodation is one built on the same lines as the farmer's sledge house, but with a greater capacity underneath the roosting com- partment,- this being used as shelter and scratching shed combined, and right well doe 3 it answer its purpose,providing the proper number of fowls are kept. If an ordinary fowl house has been in use without scratching accommodation you may easily arrdnge this either by raising the house 18 inches from the ground or by extending the roof about six feet according to the ntunber of fowls kept, and seeing that your run is- not so extensive as the farmer's it should be boarded all round two feet from the ground. This not only adds to a better appearance but breaks the cold winds, for you must remember that a few birds kept in confinement have not the same chance of keeping themselves warni as others on a free range.

BACK-YARD ACCOMMODATION.

When your space ,is beyond say ]2 yards by about 6 yards, I should,class you under the same conditions as the suburban poultry farmer, unless more than one pen were to be used, and even then complete covering in would be necessary in such a confuted area. But let me supppse for, the moment that it is your inten- tion to keep one pen of utility birds to supply you with eggs for your own household consumption, and the space at your disposal is under the dimensions already mentioned, as the majority of town back-yards are, and very often much less. In this case it is advisable to have your run entirely covered in, so that perfect cleanliness can be obtained. It is inipos;ible to keep a small run in Iroper condition unless it is covered in.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
(To be continued)

QUERIES,

CON7ITANT READER asks: In mating up a breeding pen of minorcaS how -many hens may I put with one male bird? 'and how long must I wait before saving the eggs .for sitting? ANSWiNt :—Seven hens are sufficient to one male bird if you expect good Strong progeny; but I would advise you to purchase a reserve cocker el to introduce into your pen late on in the breeding season. Providing the hens have not been previously mated this season, • you may save the eggs for sitting in about seven days. They should in that time, have got .quite settled . down Mad. sufficiently fertilised. . .

NOVICE wants to know of a good substitute for green food, as he -finds at present a great scarcity? ANSWER :—One of the best substitutes aro ordinary farm-grown marigolds. Get a good-sized one and cut it in halves. Place same in the cleanest part of your pen. You will soon find how greedily it will be devoured by your, fowls. You should have no diffi- culty in purchasing sufficient from almost any farmer at this time of the year to last you until other green food is obtainable. The following is also a good method of supplying your birds with natural green food. Get an ordinary 'seed potato box and fill with good rich soil; then sow grass and clover seeds. Make' firm and cover the top tightly over with small' mesh wire netting to keep the birds from scratching it up. When grown about one-and-a-half inches high place the box in your- poultry run to. bf eaten off. Several boxes thus prepared and sown at intervals will keep your fowls constantly supplied, and will also prove a real dainty for them.

That was the fourth site the Colonel had given them. The Church Institute was going on most satis- factorily Some £6OO had been raised but there VMS still a deficit of £l2O. The matter of school accommo- dation was a pressing one. Extensions had just been made to the National Schools, Church-strict, and important questions were pending at Fairfield. The Government had condemned the Smith-street school on , various grounds and the managers now bad before.them two schemes ohe being to purchase land adjoining,and I alter the schools to meet the Government requirements. The proposal which commended itself more strongly, although no:fi'ng definite bad been done, was to move

from he present site and build a new.girls' school, because they had perfect faith, notwithstanding all dis- couragcmentg, that they were doing their duty in building excellent day schools on their property in Church-street pnd Ellesmere-street. The Rector then

thanked the congregation for their Easter offering. "I have something to say, " h conqiuied " but the time has not yet come when I can say it. When the time comes you will see hoav deeply I have valued your kindness."

Warrington V. Salford.

Glorious summerlike weather favoured the meetieg of these teams at Wilderspool on Saturday morning. It was pleasant enough for the holiday crowd of 4,0490 which assembled. but exceedingly trying for the players and before the close there were evidences of fatigue. Neither team was at full strength, the Warrington side including six Combination players. The teams were : Warrington—Tilley, back; Fish. Dickinson, Jenkins and Landor, three-quarter tacks; ICay,and Lloyd. half- backs; A. Boardman, P. Boardman, Belton,. 'Moult, Chegters and Fairhurst, forwards. Salford—Thom, back; Cook, Thomas, Hyam and Mesley, three-quarter backs; Lomas and John bait backs; Buckler' Rees, 'Thomas, Curzon, Warwick, and Coates, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. W. Taylor, Hunslet. Touch judges, Messrs. J. I'. Hayes. Feigh, and 11: Farmer, Runcorn. Warrington soon forced play to the Salford end and Lander, after a dodgy rue, got over but the try was disallowed for some inftrmality. Soon afterwards Lander again secured from John's relieving kick, and, following up, beat Thom for pessession, and scored near the posts, Fish gosling. The famous Warrington plaser as heartily cheered on thus breaking his record established in 1906-7 of 150 points in one season.. Salford could make little headway against the superior play of the home forards, and fro:n a difficult angle Tilley dropped a fine goal. Salford improved slighly, and combination between John, Lomas, and Hyarn broke down at Mosley. The IVarriagtonians returned t the attack, Lloyd racing splendidly through the Salford backs, only tipped near the posts. Toe piessare was maintained, and after Kay and A. Boardman had been held up inches inside, Cheaters effected a great run and scored for Fish to place a goal. Half-time, Witi4ington 3 goals, 2 tries (12 points); Salford nil. Turning round, Warringtou again caused the Salford defence trouble, and after Kay and Lloyd had put in good runs, Belton got over but was recalled. The vis'tors allowed a free kick, but Lomas failed. Lloyd secured the ball, and blufEng Lomas passed to Jenkins, and the latter to Clester, who registered a try, Fish missing the goal. For a time the game was of a give- and-take nature, but eventually the Salford fore ards took advantage of weak back play by the opposition, and Mesley completed a try after smart passing initiated by Lomas. The Salford captain goaled. The visitors conthmed to force matters, and Lomas took advantage of clever work by John to score in a favour- able position for him to add the second goal. In the last minute of the game Lomas accepted a cross kick from Alesley; and, adding the goal, made the szores equal. Result :—Warrington, 3 goals and 3 tries (15 points) ; Salford, 3 goals and 3 tries (15 points).

ST. HELENS v. YORK.

At St, Helens. York early oh had to defend, and Hill feinting to pass, scored at the corner. Lilley failed to improve. Passing between Harris, Prescott and Briere resulted in the latter crossing. Lißey again failed t-b improve. Play was contested for a time in the home quarters. Kelly got away, and passed to Flanagan, who -s.zered at the'corne% Harris got his side penalised, and Plimmer opened the score for York. St. Helens repeatedly got possession, and Hill made a nice open- ing for Flanagan to score at the corner and k;ck a goal. Lavelle tumbled near his own line, and Harris scored, the goal kick failing. Near the interval Pope scored an unimproved try. Half-time score—St. Helens 1 goal and 6 tries (20 points), York 1 g0a1.(2 points). Lea started the second portion, and York at once got dangerous, and Lavell3 crossed. Plitnruer kicked a fine goal: Flanagan placed a goal for St. Helens for a York infringement. York again opened out., and tossing between Moore and Raywoo3 culminated in the latter scoring. Plirnmer kicking a fine gcal from the touch line. Following this the defence was very weak on both sides, Flanagan, Kelly and Hill crossing for St. Helens, Flanagan improving one. After Moore went through the whole of the Saints' team to score a try behind the posts, W. Wray wriggled his way over, and Plimmer was successful in placing his fourth goal. Result—St. Helens 3 goals and 9 tries (3) po'nts), York 4 goals and 4 tries (20 points).

rI3NDAY'S MATCHES

Warrington Break Records. Warrington's concluding home fixture,the return League match with St. Helens, was played at Wilderqmol on Monday before a fair crowd. The match was sensa- tional, for in the early stages Hill of St. Helens had his leg broken in attempting to prevent a try being scored, and subsequently Warrington dlspla3ed such wonderful form that they put up the huge score of 76 points to 6 —records both for the Club and for the Northern Union. Early in the game the Warrington foraards broke away with a strong dribble. and Creevey was compelled to kick over the line. The danger was removed by Toole, uho found touch near midfield. A penalty

against St,. Helens was taken by Fish, who sent outside the post. Hill was at fault in touching down and met with the accident above referred to. Thomas scored, the same player converting. Passing by the WarEngton backs was intercepted by Toole, who kicked over the Warrington line. Flanagan set off in hot pursuit, but Fish proved his equal and saved the situation. The visitors had another oppor- tunity. Creevey stoles pass intended for Bradshaw and ran to the line, where he was tackled by Tilley. Pass- ing by the Warrington backs ended in Bradshaw being thrown into touch near the line. Eventually Neill got over, Thomas failing at goal. Warringt,,n were now having matters all their own way. ,Fish scored a grand try after a splendid passing movement.. Warrington ' again pressed and Bradshaw ran over with another try, which was quickly followed by a further try from Boardman. St. Helens were helpless against the rushes of the Warrington men, their tackling being weak and ineffective. After a brilliant run by Dickenson Thomas scored and converted. lhe Warrington score grew rapidly,and further tries were scored by-Dickenson and Lloyd, goals being added by Thomas. Another round of passing saw Bradshaw dodge Flanagan and score at the corner. Thomas failed at goal. The Warrington score was further increased by Neill, who got over after the forwards had completed a brilliant movement. At half-time Warrington led by 40 points to nil. The second half was a repetition of the first. Warrington completely outclassed the Saints, who were continually on the defensive. No less than ten tries were scored, Thomas also kicking four goals. The Warrington scorers were as follows :—Thomas, three -tries; Bradshaw, three tries; Dickenson, Boardman, Shugars, and Taylor, one try each. Result :—Warring- ton, nine goals twenty tries (78 points) ; St. Helens, two tries (six points). •

LANCASHIRE SHIELD.

THIRD ROUND.

WARRINGTON "A" V. OLDHAM "A."—This match on Saturday afternoon attracted some 5,000 enthusiasts to the Wilderepool ground. Both teams had strong sides doing duty, the " Wirepullers" including Shugars, Neill and Thomas in the fors aids, whilst there were several players in the Oldham back division who had played for the senior team,Oldham were soon aggr6si re, and Thomas placed a penalty goal. Warrington rallied and after strong work Neill picked tip in the loose and scored, Thomas missing the goal. Mclntyre came near placing two penalty goals, the ball travelling.-icches wide. Warrington kept up the pressure and Thomas only kept the locals out with difficulty, Eventually Oldham improved and following smart forward work Cash scored, for Thqinas to add a goal. Warrington next pressed bard and Neill open- ing out the game, - "Lander was almost in ' on the left. Oldham's defence was superb, Thomas Earning distinction. George Thomas failed by inches at a penalty. Jordan scored, but Thomas missed the goal. Warrington continued to attack, and after Neill bad opened out the game effectively, Oldham's defence was again found wanting, and this time Lander took the ball to near the line, where D,tintith put the finishing touch to an excellent movement. Mclntyre• missed the goal. Daintith was hurt soon afterwards and took no further part in the game. In the second half the game for some time was of a give-and-take nature, and it was not until the close that the home defence showed signs of weakness, the lacks being unable to hold the advantage gained by the forwards. Oldham pressed bard in the last few minutes and scored twice, and landing a goal eventually won by three gpals three tries (15 points) to Warrington three tries (nine points).

===== 0002601_19090417_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting was held in the rooms, Friar's Green, on Tuesday evening. Mr. I'. Delaney occupied the chairand was supported by Mr. E.Plinston (secretary). There was a good attendAnce of delegates.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TELEPHONE SERVICE.

A letter was read from the Bradford District Labour Council saying that they had passed a resolution calling upon the Government to take such action its might be necessary to prevent the threatened wholesale dis- missals from the stair of the National Telephone Company,and to ensure the employment of those already discharged, the discharges being the dilect result of the action of the Government in terminating the licence of the company in 1911, thereby stopping the legitimate end necessary extension of the telephone servic,.. The letter added that in view of the greet army of the unemployed they urged upon their Council the importance of the matter, and they respectfully reinestel their 'members to adopt 'the resolution or a similar one and send it to the local magistrates, Prime- minister and Postmaster,General.

Mr. PARK ER said the National Telephone Company would do no more construction work because of the Government taking over the workings of the company. The would practically discharge their construction gang, for they were not going to do any more repairing thrto they were forced to do. Mr. Buxtnn, the Post- master-General had said that the Government would make the working of the telephone service efficient,and the people would wish they had taken it over before. He moved that they endorse the resolution of the Bradford Trades Cmucil.

Mr. PLUMB seconded add it was carried unanimously.

OLD AGE PENSIONS.

The Connell expr?ssed themselves in favour of the removal of the Poor LAW disqualification for old age pensions.

CO-OPERATIVE CONTRACTS.

Mr. PARKER moved : "That this Council send a letter to the Co-operative Society asking them to insist that in all foture cont-acts no sub-letting to outside firms be allowed." Ho said the reason he moved the resolution was became there were a lot of complaints that had been, brought to him. There were a great many un,killed men in the town out' of wotk. Thpy complained they could not get work. They had men- tioned the Co-operative Society and told him that the contractor of the new buildings had been sub-letting the pulling down to an outside firm and, it was alleged. paying the men 41d. per hour, instead of sd. the standard rate in Warringten. There should be no sub-letting in connection viith the Co-operative Society. He appealed to the delegates to insist on contractors carrying the work out themselves instead of sub-letting. A good many people who belonged to Co-operative Societies were dividend hunters and not co-operators. For his part he liked work to be done in a good way, and a good price to be paid for if. They should also insist on the standard rate of wages on future contracts. Mr. PLUMB seconded the motion.

Mr HowLisToN, in supporting the resolution, raid the members of the Stores could alter the matter them- selves. if the rnemlE,ra would attend the Co-operative meetings and bring the matter forward it would be La more effective than sending a letter from that Council.

Mr. EIALEY also supported the resolution. CrraramANsuggested to the mover the following words :—" That the standard reolgnized union rate of wages in the local district be paid on all contracts." Mr. Delaney aided that the previous resolution only dealt with unskilled men and not with the skilled. Mr. PARKEIt then moved :—" That in all future contracts the Society insist that there be no sub- letting, and a standard trade union rate of wages be paid." Mr. MEr.r4oß said that at one quarterly meeting of the Co-operative Society a resolution was passed that trade union rate of wags bo -paid. It was only the members who were to blame that they did not insist upon the resolution being carded out. He was in favour of the resolution.

The resolution was carried unanimously. FEDERATION DELEGATE'S REPORT.

Mr. PLUMB presented the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation Delegates report, in the course cif which he said that the Federation were going to make it one of their main duties to deal with unemployment. Several delegate spoke of the difficulties experienced by Trades Councils wheel trying to obtain working-class repre- sentatives on the Magisterial Benches of the country, and it was decided that the Secretary write the Labour Party's Secretary, asking that the matter be raised in the House of Commons. • Letters had also been sent to the Duke of West- minster and Lord Shuttleworth, but no reply bad been received. This showed that they had no sympathy with, the matter. •

UNEMPLOYED DEPUTATION.

Mr. PARKER referring to their deputation to the Town Cannel( re unemployment said they could not get anything of any benefit from the Town Council. They came away as they went. They had plenty of sympathy. The Town Council were very sorry that there was unemployment, but they raid they could not do more than had been done. Of course, as usual they tried to lay the blame on sornet-ody else. They had applied for .£24,C00 to ereat.3 employment, for which they were thankful. Councillor Pemberton said they might have formed bowling greens, but why had they not gone on with them? They got nothing btit apologies and sympathies. He thought his time had been very badly spent by going with the deputation. Mr. PLUMB said it was nothing but bluff tint Mr. Longdin had told them in regard to the river wall. Mr. Longdin had said that it would take 15 men a fort- night to do the work, That was untrue, and he (the speaker) considered him an obstacle in the way. The SECRETARY observed that they got a promise from the Borough Treasurer, who said that he would see that men in the borough and not outsiders would be employed in the gangs. The Sites and Buildings Committee said they were doing all in their power. The only thing he suggested was.that they should take the power from them and put it into the hands of those who would move with greater rapidity. Mr. HALEY said they could find work if they wanted but they did not want.

Mr. PLUNIII said they (the TOWU Council) were not interested in their welfare. They were interested in the property owners' welfare. They should send Trade Unionists to the To..vn Council, for they knew their needs.

DR. J. SCOTT LIMIT.

PRESIDENT OF THE WESLEYEN CONFERENCE AT PENKETH.

Dr. Lidgett,, who was given the degree of D.D. at Aberdeen University only last week, preached to a large congregation at Penketh Wesleyan Church on Sunday morning. . The eminent Nonconformist leader has a distinctive personality. A high domed forehead surmounts a lace somewhat small in comparison, but every contour and line suggests vigour and domination. There is no "whispering humbleness" about the—President of the Wesleyan Conference. He is a man of thought, wide learning and strong conviction, and he looks all this and more. Striking truths. couched in full rounded

pliraseoloav fall fro—ra his lips with oratorical fluency, accompanied by gestures which always emphasize-and never obtrude. •

On Sunday morning be spoke upon four plssages (IC St. .Matthew, 21., 10 St. John, 17 and 18., 16 St. Matthew, 2-1 and 25., 1 Phillipians, 21). Ho LIM they had been occupied in sacred and solemn memory with t he journey of the Lord Jesus Christ to Jerusalem, with His triumphant entry into the city • His passion and suffering on the Cross ; His Luria"; and now their hurts were filled with joy and gladness by His glorious resurrection. They had deep feelings of salvation by His victory over sin and death. It was a mistake to shut themselvts up even for a day, and they were not intended to find spiritual edification in treating Hini who lived, as though He were dead. How were they to understand our Lord's redemptive work? As a whole we found an inner and a lower hrilf which held together the incarnation of Christ's r surrection, the ascension and the coming of the Holy Spirit. All those things. were not mere separate events with no

inner action, but there was the inevitab)e development of the spiritual life of our Lord. The four passages together presented them with the inner history of our Lord. Jesus Christ told His disciple that He must go to Jerusalem, that Ire must suffer many things at the hands of the priests, rulers and scribes, that Ho must be milled to a cross, tliat He must be buried, and that He must riss spin. The Divine task imposed upon Him meant the self-renunciation of Himself. They did not always see the matchless spiritual strength that was in. Him. By " nnkst " it meant the bringing of Himself under a law, which was fixed. for Him. It was the law of His being. The "must" demanded that at all costs He must. go down to Jerusalem, not flinching and not turning back. Once that decisive net was taken, there, was a two-fold prospect certain. First of all there was the suffering and crucifixion in God's world and for God's Son. Suffering and crucifixion could not be the last word, for He was to be .raised up again. That must was not the must of physical necessity. There was no strain upon Him to go out, for He could have stayed in Galilee. He could have waited for quieter times to base done some patient work of teaching, instead of calling upon Himself the storm that was gathering. It had been said by critics that He tempted Providence by going to Jerusalem, and that at least, there wsts no need for Him to go up in the physical sense of corn- pulsioa. The rev, gentleman claimed that it was a voluntary act, a higher necessity, —obedience to the redemptive parpote of His Father to which He responded with filial herrt.The journey w ts not without cost. They should noses snapase the victory of Jesss was an easy one. It WaS easy no douttt (hit no pressure of temptation, no poignancy of anguish could ever cause His will to tremble. Because His will was steady and His heart was loyal, and His resolution intense it did- not mean to say that there was not sorrow upon Him that struck His heart. We saw the shadow falling upon Him as He stedfttstly st his face to go up to Je,ustlem. The pas--ion laid hold of Him on the way. It did not seem to have been present except in the background in the early days and months of His ministry. The shadow of the cross fell on John before it fell on Jesri. John knew of Christ's canting and had a glorious spiritual conception of it.He read from the fbst, divistely inspired no d .utrt, with a clear insight of ChrKt's cornirig. When Christ, had revealed his Kingdom arid was fulfilling His ministry and doing good, the anguish of the Cross came to Him. ft was not the scourging,nor the piercing of the bands and feet, that made all tha suffering on the Cross. It was the failing of His Father's love, the only influence which seemed to contralict His Father's holiness, and still more the suicidal rejection of His lore by the people". That contradiction of all that His love asked for from God, front the world, from man was the horror of the Cross. He was anxious to accept the passion rather than prove unfaithful for the rrderoption of our blood. From the moment when Christ seemed to stand helpless with Pilate, He was turning suffering into sacrifice. Ihe Cross was zit expression of spiritual life, of holy and positive consecration to His Father. That was the glory of it. By His rising from the dead His spiritual supremacy was displayed. The resurrection was the expression and proclamation to theworld, but the Cross was the great achievement. Christ said "If any man will come after Me, let him deny himself." It did not mean deny hirnsslf flesh on Goosl Friday or pleasure in Lent. The preacher would not say whether it was right or wrong. He must dens himself by affirming God in this world, and he must suffor fur it. If they would po up to Jerusalem they would have to endure many things. In conclusion the rev, gentleman implored them to follow Christ in service, lova and obedience.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
THE "CAPPING" CEREMONY AT ABERDEEN.

Aberdeen Univerg;ty, which last April conferred ete degree of Doctor of Divioity on the Clast•ical Tutor of Richmond College, Mr. Gedan, has this year, against its spring- "capping," honoured in lace manner the President of the Wesleyan Conference, who will hence- forth bo known as Dr. Lidgett. The graduation ceremony took place on Tuesday, April 6th, at noon, in the Mitchell Hall of that nvgnificent pile of granite buildings known as Marisehal College. In presenting him, Professor Gilroy said : In Rev. John Scott Lidgett we welcome a dis- tinguished graduate of the University of London, who is now one of the leaders of theological thought and Church life in oar country. He is a man difficult to

measure, more difficult, t.; appreciate ; his powers are so versatile, his industry so great, and his energy so abounding. Carlyle's definition of genius as a capacity for hard work has no more apt embodiment than in him, for he is a preacher, a theological teacher, a Church leader, the warden of a settlement, and educa- tional administrator on the London County Council, a leader of social reform, and an editor, doubtless, not without his worries. Our wonder most be how he finds the opportunity of writing those rich and , massive, but •at the some time lucid and living, theological works on which his wide reputation Ira; been built. Without undervaluing his activity in other directions, we think of him to-day mostly as a theologian, • who is moving to the front rank amongst our country's religious -thinkers. has contributed to theological literature three out- et.fmling works, marked by great width of scholarship, balance of judgment, depth of vision and religious power, and withal with a clearness of arrangement and lucidity of style that reveal his complete mastery • of his themes. These three works aro remarkably uniform in the texture of their thought and their general relation to one another, for they all hang upon and elucid fte one great seminal thought—the Fatherhood of God—in which Mr. Lidgett sees, and rightly sees, the only sa'isfying rest for the human spirit amidst the perplexities of life, and the mysteries of-providence and revelaticn, and the one perennial inspiration to. and the one abiding consolation in all our efforts after soc7al amelioration. Theological works of this character often fail in reality and power, because they are the musings of an a.:tlye and subtle, and it may be sympathetic, mind in the still and rarefied air of the study, rather than the fruit of a living contact with men, and a living knowledge of man's ills, but here, though we may agree with the author that occationally there is the sign of the busy and the hurried man, everywhere we seem to be moving in the society of one who is living amongst the deep sorrows, trials, and mysteries of life, and whose inspiration in writing is that he has a message of hope and light, by which the way of life may be cheered, if not rendered smooth. In acknowledgment of the great service Mr. Lidgett has rendered to theological thought and religious woik, we wish to offer him the only reaard we can by asking you to enrol him amongst the doctors of our University. After presentation, each geadu in-I ascended the plat- form to be' capped" and adorned with the colours representing his degree, which, in the case of those receiving the D.D., are scarlet and purple. This act was accompanied by outbursts of applause, varying in volume according to the degree of fame and popularity achieved by the recipient. The reception given to the President was most cordial and appreciative, and indi- cated that, although he is not a Scotchman, was-not educated at a Scottish University, and represented a Church which in Scotland is small and comparatively unknown, there were many, even in the laud which is the home of religion and theology, who had heard of him, and were ready to recognise the talents and work even of one hailing from over the Border.

GOOD TEAIPLARS IN CONFERENCE.

FORTIETH ANNUAL SESSION AT GLOUCESTER.

The 40th Annual Session of the Grand Lodge of England and United Services, International Order of Good Templars commenced in the Shire Hall, Gloucester, on Easter Monday. The civic decorations were . set up in the thoroughfares in honour of the visitors. The preceding meetings commenced on the eve of Good Friday with a United Conference, when Bro. J. Rowe-Brooks, P. G. Marshal, read a paper on "Our Ideal, and How to Attain it "- ; Bro. W. G. Errhigton, D.C.T. presiding.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN'S DAY

Good Friday was Children's Day. The National Juvenile Executive Council met• in the morning. In the afternoon a Juvenile procession, headed by the Civic Band, went to the Shire Hall for a "Model " Session. At night a public demonstration, was held, 15r. Hadwen presiding, and addresses being given by Mrs. E. Dimmer, G. Vice-Templar, Councillor J. Malins. J.P., G.C.T. and Bro. Prof. Franziskus Hiihnel, G.E.S., President of the German Temperance Federa- tion. The Band of Hope Union Choir. conducted by Bro. Hopkins, G. Supt. of Juvenile Work, rendered choice pieces. ,

SUNDAY SERVICES. On Sunday, Special Temperance Services were held n various places ,of worship. In the afternoon the representatives went in procession to the Northgate Wesleyan Church, when the Official Sermon was preached by the G. Chaplain, Rev. J. Craig Kelly. A Gospel Temperance Mass Meeting was held in the Corn Exchange by Officers of the International Lodge. Bro. J. W. Hopkins, International. Supt. of Juvenile Work presiding, and Bros. G. Hayler, Inter- national Electoral Supt. and T. Honeyman, Inter- national Sec. gave addresses. :::ASTER MONDAY. On Easter Monday the Grand Lodge was epened in the Shire Hall by the G. Chief Templar, Councillor Joseph Malins, J.P. A reccess followed, and deputa- tions attended from the City Corporation, The Church of England, Roman Catholics, Free Church Council ; Church of England Temperance Society ; 1.0: Rechabites ; U.K. Alliance; Western Temperance League ; British Women's T. A. ; B. of H. Union ; Gloucester Temperance Society; Y.M.C.A. ; S'unday School Union, Salvation Army, etc. each giving brief addresses of welcome to the G. Lodge. OFFICERS' REPORTS. The Officers' Reports were presented on Monday— that of the G.C.T. showing that- the membership in many districts had shrunk somewhat owing to trade depression and strikes, but the Military and Naval districts, which are not affected by trade conditions, had notably. increased. The Military Lodges num- bered nearly a hundred—most of ,them being in the Infantry, though five were in the Cavalry and eight in the Artillery. The first "Special Reserve " Lodge had been formed in connection with Mr, Haldane's new Army Scheme. Sessions had been held on troopships and a large number enrolled, and also aboard R.M.S. "Orient." The work of Captain-W. H. Collins, as Military• District Chief Templar, was warinly eulogised, as was that of the Naval District Chief Templar, Bro. J. C. Souhamy, a warrant , officer on H.M.S. " Mars," who had organised over a . score of Lodges on as inany'warehips. Admiral Lord ' Charles Beresford and other naval Commander had given every encouragement. The Grand Lodge also had a number of Lodges at distant ports and stations, including many native Lodges in _West African Settle- ments, and some in Ceylon. The report showed that, as a coloured student of the Birmingham University had been expelled from the Skating Rink at Birmingham, the G.C.T. had opposed a grant of a music license for - the Rink until the Management had " opened its doors-" irrespective of colour. The G.C.T. had induced the L.G.B. to prevent Old Age Pension Committee Meetings on licensed premises, and iteps were being taken regarding the use of such premises for Voters Revision Courts and other administrative purposes. PARLIAMENTARY MATTER. Bro. Walter Graville, G. Electoral Supt., reported the work of the Order in support of Temperance Legislation—especially for the Government Licensing Bill, the rejection of which by the Lords was strongly resented. The Order had taken part in deputations ,to Cabinet Ministers; had promoted many petit-ons to the House of Commons, and over 1,200 petitions to the Lords, praying for support of the Bill. They had promoted many hundreds of public meetings and circulated vast quantities of literature from the Grand Lodge printing works and elsewhere. The Order now welcomed the Children's At as a great reform ; and was now supporting the current bills for the closing of public-houses on election days the further curtailment of the liquor traffic on Sundays, and the Scottish Temperance Local Option Bill. THE MEMBERSHIP. • The report of Bro. Hopkins, G. Superintendent of Juvenile Work, showed 845 junior branches, with 54,331 members. Over 1,000 juniors had been drafted into the adult branches. There were also over .4,000 adult honorary and executive members in the junior branches. The junior course of study had been on Bible Temperance, and in the examination thereon 299 had 'passed •with honours, and 631 took first, 218 second and 141 third class certificates. Next year's study has to be on Temperance History. The Hon. G. Secretary Bro. E. Oliver, reported the statistical totals and accounts. The Adult section consists of 1.425 branches, with 54,756 members; and these with the Juniors -added make a total 109,506 members in 2.270 Lodges and Temples. • The following is the number of Lodges and Temples in the larger counties: Yorkshire ,132 branches with 5,323 members; Essex 127 branches witn 6,512 members; Middlesex 149 branches and 7,764 mem- bers; Surrey, 144 branahes with 7,890 members; Durham, 242 branches with 12,667 members. The G. E. Executive report awarded the Adult Numercial Challenge Shield to the Military District for an increase of 24 per cent., and the Juvenile shield to S. W. Devon, for 43 per cent. increase. The Mission work of the Order in the past year had resulted in administering the pledge to over 20,000 adialts and 27,000 juniors. The financial statements were submitted by the G. See., and by the G. Treasurer, Bro. T. V. Mills. The accounts showed a decrease in trade receipts and profits, but the apparent' deficit of nearly £4OO was fully equalled by the percentage set aside to capital account. and the value of new machinery purchased. The G. Executive had adoptect declarations against the Liquor Traders proposals for evading the 'Children's Act by providing waiting rooms for children ; protests had .been adopted, against the action of France in pressing Scamrinavian countries to lower their wine duties as a condition for floating loans in France, and the Executive also protested against the French for encouraging the Czar of Russia to veto anti-liquor legislation of Finland. PUBLIC DEMONSTRATION. A Great Public Demonstration was held on the Monday evening, the G. Chief Templar presided and the proceedings ,were taken part in by Bro. Capt. R. Rigg, High Sheriff of Westmoreland,,Ptof. Franziskns 'Ulna G.E:S.; of the German Grand Lodge, and Comm. J. Neilson, JP., G. Chief Templar of Scotland. Bro. C. H. Deavin, P.G. Organist, conducted the Gloucester Temperance Choral Union. CATHEDRAL SERVICE BY THE BISHOP On Wednesday, the Lord Bishop of Gloucester (the Right Rev.' E. C.. S. Gibson, D.D.) conducted a ' special Temperance Service in the. Cathedral, 'preach- ing a temperance sermon to the Order. AN EXTENSION PROJECT. For the further extension of the Temperance work of the Order it had been determined to raise a fund of :£5,000, toward which a niunber of substantial sub- scriptions had already been promised. . EXCURSIONS. An excursion was arranged to Ebley on Thursday afternoon, and for a round trip to Hereford., Ledbury and Malyern, on the Friday.

Nobody accustomed to SOCIALISM observe signs and AND ITS portents will be in- LEADERS. haste to regard the , resignations of Mr. KEIR HARDIE and other members of the' Independent Labour Party as final. It is at least not improbable that before long they' will all be back in their old plaes, with stronger hold on their following than before. Without 'doubt they are at once the ablest and the most necessary men in what We suppose we may call the orthodox Socialist movement. Without them 'we do not see how the Independent Labour Party is to continue a successful existence, even though it has secured such a distinguished recruit ae the Rev. R. J. CAMPBELL. It is not clear, how- ever, that its disruption would exercise any' greatly adverse influence on the representa- tion of Labour in the House of Commons, which is the chief matter of importance. The Independent Labour Party has no doubt exercised a very considerable influence on the Labour Representation Committee; but it has never obtained absolute command of it. Some of the strongest men sitting on tho Labour benches in the House of Commons are not Socialists. Even the Leader of the Party in succession to Mr. KEIR HARDIE-- Mr. ARTHUR HENDER'SON—is not a Socialist. The Parliamentary movement, for the present at any rate, will be practically uninfluenced. To that extent what has been witnessed at Edinburgh is a storm in a teacup, which will not be without its advantages, possibly, as a lesson on the subject of mutual forbearance. To those who are calm but interested spec- tators of a remarkable movement in contem- porary politics, the apparently deep cleft in what is spoken of for short as the I.L.P. Will suggest many reflections, the first of whiell is likely to be that Socialism has a confirmed habit of turning on its leaders and, so far as it can, of rending them. There is an old, formal, long-accepted phrase about the ingratitude of d emocracy. It assumes that when the people have raised men to be their leaders they soon become either angry at their virtues or jealous of their position. The case of Aristides the Just readily recalls itself front among ancient examples. That a man should be called "the Just" in contradistinction to all other men began in course of time to soon intolerable even to those by whom the title had been conferred, and ultimately assumed the aspect of a public insult for which nothing but life could atone. Such instances have been repeated, sometimes in comic and sometimes in tragic forms, from one age t° another. Your popular leader is a hero to-day and a subject of derision or a corpse to-morrow. Or so, at any rate, a cynical observer might remark, taking perhaps too deep and scornful note of the fact that in s° many instances those who have raised a man like to feel that they have the power of dragging him down again, or even t° experience the supremer satisfaction °I

doing so. Mr. KEIR HARDIE has in his time so° other leaders of the Socialist Party made the object of more bitter jealousies and worse suspicions than those which have stung 110 into much brilliancy of retort during the Easter holidays. The greatest, and we 1104 almost said the most high-minded, well, brought within the range of the Social's , movement in this country was WILLIA3S Moßfiis, who eventually found association with certain colleagues quite intolerable' mainly on the ground that they werek suspicious of his motives and his Wee:. But JOHN BURNS, surely, is the Wu- ing example of how a movement roi turn against one of its inspirers. ll°,

STRIKE SCENES AT WARRINGTON.

The strike of moulders at Messrs. Whyman's foundry still continues and on Wednesday exciting scenes were witnessed in Dallam-lane. It is state i that some twenty-six non-Union moulders were brought to the works from Scotland and started work on Wednesday. The strikers, who have been picket- ing very diligently in the neighbourhood, got to know of this and to avoid any disturbance the newly- imported workers were provided with their dinner on thq foundry premises. When they left in the evening a hostile demonstration was witnessed,Union men being present in strong force, while a thousand or more of the general public had also been attracted to the ticene. Itis alleged that missiles were thrown but no one was hurt. A detachment of police was present and the men reached their head-quarters in 'safety. A con- ference took place with representitives of the Union and ended in the visitors agreeing to return to Scot- land on their fares being paid': The money was forthcoming from the Union, and the men took their departure.

EMMANUEL CHURGH VESTRY.

The Easter vestry meeting was held on Thursday evening in the Institute, Kuutsford-road, the Rev. F. Vaughan presiding.

TUE ACCOUNTS.'

The statement of accounts showed an income of £3lO 4s. 5d.. of which about £l5O had been given at the public Fervices of the church. The Treasurer reported an adverse balance of £3B, which, consideriug the general depression and consequent greater cldims on the people, was regarded as satisfactory. By a special Easter offering to the church funds £l7 had been raised.

NOTES AND NOTIONS:,.

do not know of any principle formerly held by John BURNS which he does not hold 110 W; yet he would scarcely be safe in a meeting of any section of the Socialists. The trouble is not that Mr. BURNS has ceased to be this or that, but that he has beeome some- thing. Last week the reputation of the Labour Party in, Parliament was being trampled underfoot at the Conference of the Social-Democratic Party. The offence in this case, too, seems to have been nothing more than that appearance of importance which such a party must necessarily assume under the particular circumstances if it is worthy Of its place. Mr. KEIR HARDIE complains of insinuations that he and his friends have obtained control of the finances of the party and Of its official organ. This is merely the old story in a slightly changed form. Its repetition-is wearisome, and to many good People must be extremely disheartening. Nye are not entirely satisfied that Socialism iS a wholly beneficial or quite necessary Movement in these times; but whether it be so or not, and to however large an extent it might become so, we are quite certain that it can do injury only to itself by its hakit of shedding its leaders in a jealous temper and without cause.

The price of bread is THE INCREASED being generally raised PRICE OF BREAD, a halfpenny on the , quartern loaf. This fol- lows On similar advances, and the price will go still higher, until 7d. or Bd. is reached. More than one cause has produced this effect. The Chicago Wheat King's corner" is principally blamed; but Mr. J. A. Patten's reply is that the cause of it all is very simple. The growth in the supply of wheat is not keeping pace with the growth in the world's population. This wide- awake gentleman foresaw the short- age in November last, and he bought extensively and hoarded grain; and his sales at the enhanced price will now bring him a million pounds from his deal. The fact that large stocks do not exist in Europe to-day as they did last year means that Europe will in the next few months need bigger shipments. The Argentine crop is smaller than was expected, and the English farmer, encouraged by good prices, has sold a large proportion of his crops, and those crops are smaller than usual. But England wants fo7ty million quarters, and grows only a fifth of that total. Europe requires shipments of 1,150,000 qr. per week for the next four months, and can only see 850,000 qr. a week coming. The price of the quartern loaf is, therefore, being raised in London from 6d. to 61d., and there are even melancholy prophecies of a ninepenny loaf before the summer is at its height. Wheat, up to the 465. at Braintree, has not fetched so much for fifteen years; and a fair average price is 325. pr 345. This should give us the quartern loaf at 41d. Each rise of 4s. a quarter adds another halfpenny to the price of the quartern loaf; and if the crisis develops, and a top price of 60s. is reached, it is easy to see what this will mean for con- sumers both in rural areas and the poorer districts of the big towns.

Joan of Arc has been THE MYSTERIOUS the centre of enormous MAID. controversies lately.

• Anatole France has tried to prove that the Maid of Orleans was a scheming impostor, no better than she should be. Mr. Andrew Lang, on the other hand, has defended her character with enormous vigour; and the Pope, taking the same line, apparently, has decided to canonise her. But no sooner has the controversy about her character got into full swing than we are plunged into the middle of a controversy about her birth. • Mr. Stuart Glennie, an English- man settled in Italy, has produced a book contending that the Maid was not even a Frenchwoman! People have often wondered, says he, whence Joan got her complexion and her free-and-easy manner of conversing with kings and princes. They have also marvelled at her strength in climbing siege-ladders at Orleans in heavy armour when only seven- teen years of age. The first difficulty he settles by saying—and substantiating (to his own satisfaction)his statement by references —that Joan was really an Italian,daughter of an exiled member of the noble family of Ghislieri, of Bologna. The second difficulty melts away when he avers that the Maid was born not in 1414, but in 1404, and was consequently twenty-nine years of age when her career closed in so terrible a way. Of course-, one must not believe everything in the way of new historical theories. One man has proved that Napoleon was a Solar Myth! But it is always on the cards that long- standing delusions may exist and be destroyed by impartial-nfinded though little- known men. Be this as it may, we advise Mr. Stuart Glennie not to go too near Paris. Joan has (as the recent riots testify) admirers there, and some of them might feel inclined to refute his arguments with bludgeons.

An American father, after reading his son's school report, remarked: "Really, Henry, this is most unsatisfactory. Why, do you know, at your age George Washing- ton was at the top of his class ?" Henry's reply was: "Yes, father; and at your age he was President of the United States.— "Daily News." . * * It is recorded in connection with the Postal Clerks' Conference this week that in a certain rural village a man carrying a ham in a basket entered the local post office to purchase a stamp. " Who is your ham for ?" asked the sub-postmaster in the way of friendly chat. " For Mrs. --," was the , reply. "It is very perticklar," cause they're expectin' friends to stay, and —" 'You can take it back home wi' you," said the sub-postmaster. "Their company's not coming. They had a postcard this morning to say as how the kids had the whooping cough." * *

The Rev. Henry Woods Perris, brother of Mr. Alfred Perris, Warrington, will appear in his old pulpit at Cairo-street Unitarian Chapel to-morrow (Sunday). It is 31 or 32 years since the rev, gentleman left War- rington, and 20 or 25 years , since he last preached at Cairo-street, but there are very many residents of-Warrington who still have a vivid recollection of his five years ministry here. He took a very prominent part in the intellectual life of the town, and no doubt those who knew him will be glad of the opportunity to see and hear him again. * * *

The statement that our naval supremacy has gone is naval nonsense.—Mr. Lambert, M.P.

Our system of examination has become the greatest curse to the education of this country.—Sir John Gorst. Every girl-looks forward to being a wife, and she should learn how to make a home.— Miss Cleghorn, at the Teachers' Conference. One needs to pass into the depths of influenza to understand some of the verses in Job, Lamentations, and the Psalms.— Bishop Thornton. * * *

"An Englishman's Home" is to be with- drawn from the stage in Berlin, and we are quite frankly glad that it has met with so cold a reception in that city. Its fate is not due to any political cause, as the criticisms of the German Press make clear. The "North German Gazette " says : "We Ger- mans have too high an opinion of the intelli- gence of the British people to believe for a moment that such stupidity is possible in the home of a single English family." Another paper speaks of the author of the play as having "the soul of a Boxer." We may hope that the cold common-sense of the German Press may do somathing to convince John Bull bow foolish he looks when he goes scarernongering. * * *

The process of revision which the Tariff of the United States has been undergoing shows how the Tariff Reformer's ideal of a "scientific tariff" works out in practice. The "Morning Post," the ablest organ which the cause of Tariff Reform possesses, has been candid enough to publish its American Correspondent's criticism of the manner in which such tariffs are framed. It is, he points out 4 only the interests of the different trades which obtain any representation in the inquiry. The general interests of - the community have no representative,and there is no one to speak up for the consumer. "A scientific tariff" is an attractive phrase, but in practice it works out as a 'tariff shaped by the pressure of the most powerful vested interest. *

Most of the holiday weather gave us a welcome foretaste of summer, with a . broad hint of some of its drawbacks. For two classes the holiday was not an unmixed delight—the walkers and the cyclists. Although there was little wind, the dust lay very heavily on the roads, and the passage of the motors stirred this up into dense clouds, which were long in settling. Indeed, on some of the popular highways a dense fog of dust prevailed almost through- out the day, and the only consolation that the harassed pedestrian had was the thought that the occupants of the cars must have passed almost as bad a time as they plunged through the white cloulds left by the pre- ceding cars. If the motor_ car is ever to become an object of other than dislike to those who do not ride in it, some means will have to be found of dealing with the dust nuisance. * * *

Advocates of Tariff " Reform " talk glibly about the advantages of taxing the foreigner," and so raising the revenue for this country. Recently, however, an object lesson has been given of how a tariff would affect British industries. Mr. W. J. Noble, senior partner in the firm of W. J. Noble and Son, cardboard box manufacturers on a large scale at Pudsey, in conversation with a Press representative, said that within the list few days he had been taking a large contract with a Continental firm for goods which formed the raw material for his business. In the course of the negotiations he received a letter from the London agents of the Con- tinental firm stating that— Should any alterations take place in the fiscal laws of England, and an impost be placed on these goods, an additional price equal to the impost must be paid by the purchaser. Will the foreigner have to pay the fax in a case like this? * * *

Sir Donald Currie, whose death occurred on Tuesday,. 'was one of the greatest figures in the shipping world. He was a man of bold and enterprising ideas and of great business capacity. It was through his exertions that the Castle Line, from a small beginning, became one of the greatest ocean- going lines of the country. Sir Donald had so long retired from Parliamentary life that his death will have no effect in the political world. He was elected for Perthshire as a Liberal in 1880, and in 18851 e was returned for West Perthshire. When the great split in the Liberal party on Home Rule occurred he became a Unionist, but this did not seriously affect his personal relations with Mr. Gladstone. Several times he took the Liberal leader for a cruise .on one of his fine steamers, and perhaps the most interesting of these trips was the sail to the. Baltic in 1883. Not only Mr. Gladstone, but Lord Tennyson were on board, and at Copen- hagen they met Queen Alexandra, the Royal family of Denmark, the Czar and Czarina, and the King and Queen of Greece. It was on this occasion that Lord Tennyson, at Queen Alexandra's request, recited one of his poems. There was no man who more regretted the Boer war than Sir Donald Currie. He believed that the difficulty might have been _solved by patient diplomacy. lie knew Mr. Kruger well, and has' related that he went to Mr. Chamberlain and warned him that though he might persuade Paul Kruger, he could never drive him. When the Presi- dent of the Transvaal issued his ill-fated ultimatum to this country, Sir Donald

recognised that there was no other course open but to fight the issue out. * * * Mr. John Drury, whose life story we publish in another column is one of the most venerable and respected figures in Warring- ton. Although he has almost attained his eighty-fifth birthday, he i; remarkably active and thinks nothing of sallying forth alone on visits to relatives and friends in different parts of the town. He has retained all his faculties and talks most intelligently on public affairs. To hear him recount his entertaining reminiscences of the past is a pleasure, and a perusal of his autobiography will show that he has had an uncommonly varied career. Those who have known him only as a temperance reformer and Christian worker will doubtless be surprised to learn that in his younger days he served in the army, but such indeed is the case and he is proud of his short experience of regimental life in Ireland.

Death of Miss Kate Gittens.

Many. of our readers will regret to hear of the death of Miss Kate Gittens, the second daughter of the late 'Mr. Edward W. Gittens of M inche'ster-road, Warring- ton, which took place at The early age of 33 years, on Easter Sunday; at the Friedenhem Hospital, Swiss Cottage, London. Mi;s Gittens, who W.lB the matron of the Sanatorium, Malvern, was well known in Wallington where she had many friends. Whilst in Warrington she was a regular attendant at the Parish Church, and for some years was a teacher in the Sunday school. Although suffering from a very painfulinternal complaint she remained at duty until five weeks ago when she was removed as a private pafent to the hospital where she died. The deceased was sister-in-law ta Mr. Councillor Steel, and many letters of sympathy and condolence have been received by the family from. various parts of the. country. The body was removed from London by rail on Thursday on which day the funeral took place at the 'Warrington Cemetery. The Rev. Canon Willis conducted the service in the church and at the graveside there was a large attendance of friends aia.cl sympathisers. A fire which foramately did not attain serious dimensions broke out on Friday midnight at the works of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co, Ltd. Noticing a glare, P.C. Bartley informed the lodgeman, and it was found that a fire had originated among a number of packing cases.and some straw adjoining a corrugated iron building full of wooden patterns. A number of people living in the vicinity were aroused, and Mr. Holmes the under-manager of the works, prozeeded to the scene. The works fire appliances were got into action and did good work until the Warrington Fire Brigade arrived with the steamer "Captain." The fire had spread to a portion of the building, but the smart work of the brigade, directed by Superintendent Turner, prevented any further extension. There was a good supply of water, and after an hour's work the ilamcs were extinguished, but not before a quantity of patterns had been destroyed or damaged. Mr. W. 11. Rudie, the' secretary of the' company, and Mr. I'. Bowers, the works manager, were present during the operations of the brigade. The cause of the fire is unknown, as the watchman

by special plant.—J.S. Bridge st.—Advt. Easter Holidays.

ARLAND, Ironmonger,

The glorious summerlike weather which marked the cpening of the Easter Holidays continued until Sunday afternoon when there was a shower of rain, and altogether the outlook for Bank Holiday was unpromising. Monday opened threateningly and during the morning there was a heavy downpour of rain which cast a gloom over the spiiits of the holiday makers. Shortly after noon, however, there was a great improvement, the clouds rolled away, and the sun shone brilliantly. On the whole the holiday was a great success and tripper's spent a happy thne at the valiong seaside ancr rural resorts. For those who remained at home, the football match Warrington v. St. Helens, was a great attraction and in the evening there were crowded houses at the various places of amusement. The following are the returns from the railway companies as to the bookings for God Friday and Easter ftfonday :—London and North-Western, Chester 100; North Wales, 100; Frodsham, 330; Liverpool, 50; Blackpool, 200; Morecambe, Windermere, &c., 60; Scotland, 60 ; East C .ast, &c, 30. Cheshire Lines, Manchester, 2,000; Liverpool, 2,200; New Brighton, 800; Southport, 900: Belle Vue, 100, DRESS BASKETS, TRAVELLING TRUNKS, etc. Large stock, reasonable prices.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st.—Ad% t. Death of a Local Choirmaster. Many of our readers will regret to hear of the death of Mr. John Gibbins, 25, Green-street, which took place with painful suddenness at Bispham, near Blackpool, on Thursday evening. Mr. Gibbins, who was forty-five years of age, was a native of Warring- ten and worked as a pattern-maker at Pearson and. Knowles' until a few months ago, when ill-health caused him to relinquish his employment. He went to Blackpool to recuperate and rettfrned about two months ago apparently greatly benefited. He continued to make good progress under the care 'of Dr. John Bligh, of Liverpool, and on Saturday last he again journeyed to Blackpool and stayed with 4 brother at Bispham. So late as. Thursday evening his wife received an encouraging letter and on Wednesday Mr. Gibbins had enjoyed a bicycle ride of some five miles, yesterday a serious message summoned Mrs. Gibbins to Blackpool and on arriving she found that her husband had passed away the previous night. The sad news was received with the deepest regret in the Bank Quay district where Mr. Gibbins was well-loaown and highly esteemed. He took a great interest in music and for fifteen years had conducted the choir of the Church of the Sacred Heart, which he had succeeded in bringing to a high state of efficiency. Much sympathy is felt with the widow in her bereavement.
