PUBLIC BENEFIT UNDER TILE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHI-RE.—Advt. London and Manchester Industrial Assurance Company, Ltd. Wedding at St. Barnabas' Church.

There was a pretty 'wedding at St. Baniabas' Church on Wednesday afternoon when Mr. Thomas Evans, son of Mr. Thomas Evans, 80, Folly-lane (organist at the church), Was married .to Miss Adelaide Rastric Daniel,of Ashton-under-Lyne. The weather was beautifully fine and a large congr.eg,ation assembled to witness the ceremony. The Vicar (Rev. J. H. Cr. Hollingworth) officiated. The choir, of which the bridegroom is a member, was in attend— ance, and the hymns" The Voice that breathed o'er Eden," "How welcome was the call." and " 0 perfect love" were sung. Mr. T. G. Reeves was at the organ and played appropriate selections including the "Bridal March "from Lohengr, in " (Wagner) and" Wedding March (Mendelesohn.) The bride was attired in a' pretty dove coloured dress, trimmed with white lace and silk, with hat to match, and carried a lovely bouquet of tulips, lilies and. smilax, the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were the Misses Cissie and Eva Evans (sisters of the bridegroom) and the Misses Amy and Bessie Berry, of Leigh, who wore white embroidered dresses with green silk sashes. The Misses Evans wore tuscan hats, trimmed with white silk and forget-me-nots,and. the Misses Berry had black hats. All carried bouquets of daffodils and smilax, the gifts of the bridegroom_ Mr. Harold Gilchrist, of Biddulph, Staffordshire, dis- charged the duties of best man. After the ceremony, a reception was held in St. Barnabas' school and was largely attended. Later the happy couple left for Colwyn Bay, where the honeymoon is being spent; the bride's travelling dress being a navy blue Norfolk costume with hat to match. Many handsome presents were received.

14IEDICAL OFFICER OF HEALTEI SUNIMONED. At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesdhy,Dr..T. Cooto Hibbert, Medical Officer of Health for Warrington, was summoned for being the owner of a dangerous dog.— Mr. Browne who represented defeuthot said he dented that tile dog was dangerous. Constable Woods said he was on duty at -11.45 a.m. in Grappenhall on 3rd March when he saw a dog chasing sheep. It worried one of the sheep. Mr. Browne said the dog was with two ladies, Mrs. Hibbert and a friend of hers. Mr. Browne (to the constable) :—Were not these ladies in sight when you went inside the field :—They were about 200 yards away. _ _

M-r. Browne: The lady who was with Mrs. Hibbert was standing in the first gateway ?—No. Mr. Browne remarked that, the lady said that the dog never ran the sheep away at all, that the dog never went near the sheep end that they only ran away when the constable hit the dog.—This the con- stable denied. Dr. llihbert was then called and in answer to Mr. Browne said he had had the dog two months and that it had always teen under control, and had never run sheep before—at any rate not since be bad bad it. The dog had never been out of the house without someone in charge of it. Mr. Basket-vine, Whitley, the previous owner of the dog, said he bad it about 3 or 4 months and sold it to Dr. liibb(rt last January. It never ran sheep while witness had it, and it had many opportunities of doing so, for he used to take it through the lanes where the sheep were.. _

M. Hibbert said that whilst out sbe suddenly noticed that the dog ha i disappeared. On looking round she saw the policeman coming .out of the road. She went en ahead to see where the dog was and left Mrs. Mathias behind. She saw the policeman jump over the gate and immediately afterwards the dog jumped out. Witness said the constable told her that the dog had half killed a sheep. He pointed to the sheep and said it was over there. Witness could not see anything tho matter with the sheep.. . _ _ . - _

• Mrs. -Mathias said she was with Mre.Aibbert and when the dog was missed she jumped on to the gate to see whera it was. The dog was near the sheep but was not running them. The sheep were ouite quiet. She could not see anything the matter with the sheep. The policeman had said he had hit the dog, but it was not at all sore.

Mr. Browne addressing the Bench said the case was of a trivial nature. The dog had no feeling or any animus against the sheep. Because that dog unfortun- ately went into the field where sheep had been pre- viously worried it was assumcd by the police that more would be worried.

The case was disroisFed on payment of costs.

WARRINGTON .BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Bond of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, the Rev. T. R. Matthews presiding. There were also present :—Rev. M. Ryan, Dr. Murray, INTessrs. G. Mather, J. BillingeP W. Gill, A. J. Jolley, A. Brooks, W. C. Brooks, M. W. Seton, J. Cook, NV. Flan nag3n, NV. Houghton, R. Forshaw,- R. Bolton, E. L. Bent, A. Bottomley -(clerk), R. W. EXTRA REM UNER AT lON. - At a meeting of the Finance Committee the Clerk read a letter from the secretary of the Royal Com- mission cn the Poor law and Relief of Distress enclosing cheque value £7 13s. 01, stating that the Treasury at the instance of Lori George Hamilton, chairman of the Commission, had placed at the dis- posal of the Commission a sum of money for tlie purpose of distribution amongst the Poor Law authorities in the United Kingdom who were good enough to furnish statistical information for the Commission. Tie committee c,nsidered the work done by various officers in compiling the information and the extra remuneration granted to them, and decided to pay the £7 13s. to Mr. R. W. Peacock, assistant clerk, for.his services in connection with the return.

TAXATION OF LAND VALUES.

The CLERK read a letter from the Clerk to the Poplar Guardians asking thv Board to support a resolu- tion urgently requesting that provision be made in the forthcoming Budget for a substantiartax on land table, both occupied and unoccupied, such tax to be used for the purpose of lightening the burden of the rates by providing out of the national funds for the necessary provision for the needs of the aged and infirm. It was further uquested that measures should be taken to secure the valuation and taxion of land values in such a manner that the great landlords should be compelled to bear their share of the cost of those public improve- ments which so largely and continually increased their rents.

Before reading the letter the CLERK said he thought the Board would decide that it was a political matter. Mr. BENT moved that the letter lie on the table. Mr. FLANNAGAN seconded and the motion was agreed to.

ANNUAL 11 EETING .

The annual meeting for the election of chairman and vice-chairman was axed for Wednesday, the 21st inst.

VOTES OF THANKS.

Mr. BILLINGE moved that a vote of thanks be accorded to the Chairman for his services during the past year. He said they would all he agreed that Mr. Matthews had conducted the business very expedi- tiously, and he had been specially endowed with those diplomatic qualities which made a successful chairman. The result a as that their work had bren very harmoni- ously and efficiently carried through. Mr. Billing° referred to the change in the clerkship which bad taken lace during the year, and after paying a tribute to Mr. button, said Mr. Bottomley had displayed business-like tact and aptitude, and under his able guidance the Board felt quite safe. They were fearing that when the report of the Poor Law Commission came out, they would

almost be wiped out of existence or turned adrift, but they felt that in the Chairman they had one who, when they were stranded, would brine them to the desired haven. During the year they had experienced an excep- tional amount of destitution brought about by depres- sion in trade, and the cases which had arisen had diminished the exchequer of the Union. They had, however, been efficieutly and systematically investigated and relieved. Mr. Matthews closed his year of office in love and charity with his colleagues, and he had been an ideal persomlity in the chair. Mr. A. BnooKs, in seconding, aaid the whole Board was thankful to 1%1 r. Matthews for the manner in which he had conducted the work. They did not forget that during the year he had been very nigh unto tbe valrey of the shadow' but they all rejoiced that be was with them to help them in municipal affairs. Messrs. Bent, Mather, Jolley and loorshaw, and the Rev. M. Ryan supported the motion which was carried with applause. The CHAIRMAN said ha thanked them all very march for their kind remarks but be thought too much had been said ("No, no.") He bad enjoyed the year's work very much; it appeared to have been a peaceful, corn, fortable and harmonious year. Of course, he had not bad the full benefit of the year's chairmanship, but that was no fault of his own. Still, when be had been able to attend be had enjoyed the business and the associa- tion vary much and he thanked them all most sincerely for their kind words concerning him. Thirteen years had passed since Ile became a member of the Board and at the end of that time he felt as happy and as juvenile at those meetings as he did when he joined the Board. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN then moved a vote of thanks to the vice-chairman—" the übiquitous and versatile.. Mr. Mather."

Mr. A. Brtooxs seconied and said the breezy and g(nial way in which Mr. Mather bad conducted the business when rallod upon made his succession secure. The vote was heartily accorded and Mr. MATHER responded.

The groat festival of Easter Day was fittingly cele- brated at the Parish Church on Sunday and large congregations joined in the joyous s-crvices. The church with its rich decorations of spring flowers pre- sented a beautif.il picture. There we: e four celebrA- tions of the Holy Communion and the conimunicants numbered 851. At the evening service the magnificent "Hallelujah chorus" was splendidly, rendered as an anthem, and at eight o'clock there 'VEII3 a vocal and org•in mital, Mr. A. Greenough being the vocalist. Bewsey-road Wesley Guild.

There are many -things in the Wesley Guild which can be commended, and the fact that there is a feeling of real brotherhood and sisterhood existing amongst the members is one worthy of record. Although the season of the Guild is over, they are to be kept •in touch with one another during the summer. They,have arranged a cycle club and the first of the runs took place last Saturday when about thirty young peop'e set off for Rostherne. Like most youn; people they were not exactlyessatisded with the comparatively short distance travelled, so they spun along until they reached Booth Bank, where they bad an enjoyable tea. The entity? was voted a capital ono an! Warrington was reacb;d about 9 o'clock. Although the weather was inclement on Easter Monday the members and friends of the Guild realised that it was Bank Holiday* aad that they must have a good time - somehow. They decided on a ramble to Moore, hut the "ramble to Moore," had to be a ramble on the train. However, "everything comes to him whn waits," and the fine weather clme when they steamed into the station. Arrived there they went for a long walk into the country. Forgetting the laws of the land, "that nobody shall pity football in a pub- lic road," the 3 oung men punched a ball about with, great glee. Tea was served in the Wesleyan school- room and afterwards they we t to Miss Bessie Dutton's house, where a devotional meeting was held. After this croquet and other games were indulged in. The party " rambled " home in earnest at 7.30, after .a verb enjoyable outing.

Rev. E. B. Keeling on Modern England.

The Rev. E. B. Keeling preached at BeTvsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday. In the evening he took his text from Ist Corinthians' Ist chapter, 45-47 verses. In the course of his sermon he stud the lower life had the start of the higher. Tke37 never saw anything refine' in the tastes and inclinations of a baby. As one lookod-over the life of England to-day, and as they read the paper, and as they looked on the direction of popular thinking and popular speaking and all the rest of it, it looked uncommonly as if we were in the baby stage. What did a rich man care aboat ? Why, getting a bit richer, and when he did get richer he looked mainly aft“ his bodily conifort. That was the cry of the people to-day. For a little more to eat and drink and pleasure they would do almost anything. They talked about eight hours a day for work, eight hours a day for pleasure, and eight hours a day for steer*. He did not grudge them those things' but he should like earnestly to know a little bit of something Weber than the demands of our body. Just think of the ' tremendous energy that was used in this country to-day—the manufacture of machinery and,the work that machinery had to do when it was made; the building of vessels, the engineering of them, and all the trades that were concerned with machinery. What was it all for? There must be some great end to be served by that gigantic energy. We were all spoiling the climate with smoke, we were spoiling the conutry with streets, we were making nature hideous and we were working like nriniacs all the day. What was it for? That men might ba fed and clothed. Men wanted that which was natural first, and afterwards that which was spiritual. If one hundredth part of the energy that went to making clothing and bread were given to the salvation of the race, just think of the change that would bike place. Still nobody MIS ever content with those things and nobody ever had been. The rich had learnt the deceitfulness of riches. There was no need for them to work; their income was assured • but they' must have some exciting amusement, or .life would become tedious. That meant that riches could do next to nothing to provide satisfaction. Man could not, be content with mere earthly things. There was no higher compliment fore Christian than to be called &Christian. The men in the slums knew when a man was good. Look at, Christ's life end they could see it at work. If anyone could preach to-day as Jesus Christ preached, there would be no empty pews, and there would be no buildings big enough. It was a curious thing that the capacity for God came out. The old life had to be got under if the new life were to thrive. A Man must be prepared to have his tastes, and his whims, and his fancies knocked to smithereens if he were going to be a Christian.

• Speaking in the drill-hall, Carlisle, on Tuesday 'evening, Mr. Ure, the Lord Advocate, 'made some striking allusions to current politics. Referring to trade depression and unemployment,- he said the perfect cures for this must be left to the quacks and the - charlatans. of public life. (kar, hear.) All thoughtful .men had now seen that, although there were many palliatites, 'many temporary expedients, to tide us over emergencies, they were only palliatives after all, and we had not yet reached the very root of -the mischief. Thoughtful men, to whatever political party they belonged, any Government of whatever _party in this country, must concentrate their attention upon the problem of unemployment, and must endeavour to reach the heart of the problem. The

CAUSES OF UNEMPLOYMENT were many and various, and he suspected that the remedies must be many and various too. That was the belief of his Majesty's present Ministers, and they were determined before they left office to do what in them lay to find out same permanent remedy for this want bf employment amongst our own people. (Cheers.) The main lines on which they must proceed were very clearly marked out for the Government, he thought, but he observed that their political opponents were going to the country at the next general election on their remedy for lack of employment. For the first time in the memory of the oldest politician he was now addressing, the Tory party had a constructive policy Avhich they were going to lay before the country—Tariff Reform (laughter and cheers.) Tariff Reform was now presented in two entirely separate and distinct shapes. "It is presented to you," he continued, "by the leaders of the Tory party, Mr. Balfour and his colleagues, in one shape ; it is pre- sented in a very different shape by the

BIB:ZEN-THROATED TUB THUMPERS (laughter), who I see at street corners whenever a by-election is in progress, and wherever two or three are gathered together" (laughter and cheers), Mr. Balfour and his colleagues believed in the efficacy of legitimate foreign competition, Which. acted as a stimulus to British trade. They would exclude th 3 foreigner, , but their remedy was small and wide- spread duties, which were to supply the revenue that was to give 'old-age pensions and other social reforms, 'which- they dangled before the eyes of the people. Small duties was a relative term, but if these duties ,were to he allowed they would embrace, at the least computation, 2,009 different articles. On a moderate estimate it would cost the country about 31 millions to collect the duties. and it would get out of them about five millions. So foe a million and a half they were. to turn .upside down the whole trade of the country. The common people of this country would-not now submit to taxes being laid on the necessities of life (cheers). The Lord Advo- cate contended that this taxation would brit ig a large burden upon the common people, and he added, "I wonder what. the dukes and marquises and earls, who met among my .fellow- citizens the other day; would say if they had to sub- mit to what was tantamount to an additional income tax of Bd. or 10d. in the pound." if that were so the patriots would, squeal, and we should hear very little of extra Dreadnoughts being built. (Laughter and cheers). But Mr. Balfour's Tariff Reform was not that which won by-elections in England, never in Scotland. (A. Voice:, "'What about GlAsgow ?") It did not win it there. His friend Mr. Scott-Dickson was too wise a man to believe in this nonsense, and his fellow-citizens knew too well what they were about. ("But you cannot get .over the fact that you were beaten," shouted another member of . the audience). "But," remarked Mr. Ure,"We Were not beaten by Tariff. Reform." (Cheers.) The other section of Tariff Reformers were those who wanted to • keep the foreigners out, do all the work in this country, and then would come the millennium—to-day or to-morrow. (Laughter and a Voice : "That's not so.") If these noisy gentleman had- their, way the last stage would be worse than the first. Mr. Ure then' entered into a long detailed .argumeht in favour 'of the

TAXATION OF :LAND VAIXES.

le contended that there should be set down in the rate book the true market value of the land, and the owner's contribution to the rates should be in exact proportion to its market price. They in Scotland sent up a bill to the Lords on the land, question, and it emerged transformed into a piece of harmless imbecility. - (Laughter.)."_ Was the country to - wait. indefinitely until those, gentlemen had mastered the land question? Were ,they to wait for those laggards? "I think," he proceeded; "We must get it somehow, and I believe most of us know a way by which we can dispense with those gentlemen's services. (Cheers). I am not saying at this moment whether that course will be taken or not • and I am not in the secrets of my friend the Chancellor of the Exchequer. Even if I were I know my duty too well to disclose them to you to-night. The position of one like myself, who is inside the Government, but outside. the Cabinet, is singularly embarrassingg- He , has to put a bridle on his tongue. What -.th Government asked for was that the House of Lords should give the two-party system fairplay. 'Let them' do . for the Liberals just the same as they did for the Govormrient which pre— ceeded them. (Hear, hear). But what the present Government meant to do was to restore complete and absolutely 'untouched the ancient rights and privileges .of the freely-chosen representatives of the people." (Cheers).

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

RIVALLING THE DIAMOND'S HARDNESS. Tantalum is a serious rival of the diamond OA regards hardness. In an attempt made to bore a hole through a tantalum plate a dia- mond drill was Osed, revolving 5,000 times ,per minute. After continuous drilling for seventy hours it was found that a small dent about 100th of an inch deep had been,made; it was a doubtful question which substance had suffered the greater damage.

TELEPHONE WIRES ON ICE.

In laying the telephone wires to the Regina Margherita Meteorological Observatory on Mount Rosa it was found impossible to use either the overhead or underground method. The snow and glacier ice prevent the latter, and the fear of storms the former. On the other hand, an insulated cable would sink in the ice if laid on it. So the plan has been tried of laying the wire on the ice itself, trusting to this for insulation. This is the highest telephone line in Europe, the obser- vatory standing at a height of 14,5981 t. DEPTH AT WHICH MINERS CAN WORK. Below 501 t. the temperature rises in the .proportion •of ldeg. for every 65ft. of depth, except where currents of water carry the heat away. The result is that at a depth of about 4,000 ft. we reach a temperature of 98deg., or blood heat. This renders it exceedingly diffi- cult to work coal pits below that depth. This is the reason that Great Britain's Coal Com- mission decided that mines are not workable below 4,000 ft. The thickness of the solid rocks building up the crust of the earth is .at least thirty to forty miles: At that depth the heat is such as would reduce everything on the surface of the earth to liquid. But the pressure of the overlying rocks is so great that until the relation of the heat to the pressure is known it cannot be said whether the earth at that depth is fluid or solid.

THE WESLEY GUILD.

Conference at Manchester.

[SPECIAL REPORT.]

The Wesley Guild Constitution received the sanction of the Wesleyan Methodist Conference which met at Liverpool in 1893, and the Guild has now nearly com- pleted its thirteenth year as an organic part of the Church. It owes its inception to the disquiet occasioned by the failure of the church to retain its young people in its fold. The state of affairs in the early nineties led to the petitioning of Conference and to the appoihtment of a committee to enquire into the whole question and formulate a scheme. The Rev. C. H. Kelly was the impelling force and at his invitation the Rev. W. B. Fitzgerald read a paper before the committee describing an institution he had organized at Roscoe Palace,Leeds,desigued to accomplish the very objects that the Conference desired. The committee were so impressed with the work of Mr. Fitzgerald that, with one or two minor alterations, the scheme he outlined was recommended to Conference and adopted. From that time the progress Of the Guild numerically intellectually and spiritually has been steady and con- tinuous; it is a Methodist institution and its objects are to make young Christians, to make Christians work, and to make young Christians work to the best advantage. I cannot now attempt to relate the Complete historical story of the Wesley Guild but it is a story well worth the telling. Realizing that the best results are to be obtained from the free interchange of ideas the Central Council, with the Connectional Secretary, Mr. Fitzgerald, have arranged biennial conferences of workers. The first held at Sheffield was attended by over five hundred representatives from all parts of Great Britain, the second conforence just con- cluded in Manchester brought together 720 delegates from places so far- apart as Aberdeen in the* North, Newquay and the Channel Isles in the South and from the Eastern seaports to the Isle of Man. The Conference proper began on Easter Monday morning but delegates commenced to arrive on Saturday morn- ing and the local Committee, with the Rev. H. Mudie Draper at their head, had made capital arrangements to fill up the interval in ways profitable both to the individual and to the organization as a whole. ...On Saturday afternoon the magnificent "John Rylands " Library was thrown open and the chief librarian, Mr. H. Guppy, delivered a lectUre on Books before printing," taking the mind back through the ages to the dim and misty days when the centre of civilisation was in the East, the days of Babylonism, of Persian and Egyptian power. In the evening the Deputy-Mayor of Manchester, Councillor Harrop and Mrs. Harrop received the delegates and friends in the new Whitworth Art Gallery; a splendid opportunity was thus afforded for the making of friendships and the renewal of those formed two years ago in Sheffield. Previous to the reception there had been a meeting of District Secretaries, under the chairmanship of the Rev. W. J. Moulton, M.A., who discussed the important work of district organization. Your correSpondent not being a .District Secretary cannot relate the proceedings but he understands that many points were considered for perfecting the in tchinery. , •

-On Easter Sunday the churches in the Manchester and Bolton Districts had arranged special services in view of the visit:of the Guilders. At many Holy Com- munion was. celebrated at eight. o'clock - arid so on Easter Day some hundreds of young Methodists united in the Sacrament in remembrance of the Lord's death till His coming again. An afternopn Musical and devotional service under Mr. Fitzgerald's presidency was a time of great • spiritual .refreshing. The topic was "Out and out Christianity," Mr. Bowen of Leigh, Arr. Gregory of Aldershot, Miss Davies of Finchley and the Rev. F.J. Pope of London, speaking on "Belief," "Enthusiasm," Work" and "Loyalty to the Church." The time was all too short for the number who had testimonies to give after the lead of the above speakers. - Monday morning found the .Central. Hall filled by 9 o'clock for a devotional 'service. The Rev. T. Ferrier Hulme, M.A., -gave an address on "Our 'Field of Labour" directing the minds of :his hearers to the high and sacred trust confided to them And invoking the blessing a the Heavenly Father upon the Con- ference. At ten o'clock the business session com- menced, Mr. Russell Roseveare, of Plymouth, read a piper on "THE COMPREHENSIONS OF THE GUILD."

He spoke on the wisdom of preventing overlapping and of seeing that the energies were not frittered away in unprofitable channels. There was, he said, a field ' of labour so large, so fertile and so full of possibilities as to inspire them to the highest consecration and service. As Methodists they must work in and through their Church, and their ambition must be to keep the Church worthy of its high calling and noble tradition and to increase its efficiency. Alluding to the zeal of youthful members the speaker submitted that it was imperative to put them to definite work at the risk of a few mistakes. He thought the Guild an ideal institution for creating a sense of civic and national responsibility, and urged the importance of the Methodist Union for social- service. Mr. Roseveare deplored the spirit of commercialism that pervaded present day football. He recognised that it was a fine, healthy recreation, which 4.1, young man should be able to enjoy without, getting into doubtful company. —Mr. E. S. Baynes initiated a discussion, many speakers bearing witness to the philanthropic, social and spiritua work being done throughout the country.—The Rev. H. Bisseker commended special evangelistic services for the more highly educated young people. He did not criticise the methods of present evangelistic work. His life in London had made him a staunch believer in such work. But he knew there were types of people who were not touched by these methods and there was more than one entrance into the Christian life. The Rev. W. J. Moulton MA., summed up'the discussion in the form of a resolution urging the necessity of all Guilds taking the widest and most comprehensive view of the field of service. Mr. David Rycroft. of Blackpool introduced the subject of the Junior Guild pleading for careful direct teaching for the children according to their capacity and the gradual and progressive training of them for a place in the Church in later years. An animated discussion followed which brought out many helpful suggestions. TIIE PRESIDENT OF THE CONFERENCE

the Rev: Dr. Scott Lidgett., then gave an address. His seceptiOn was " rousing," despite adverse critieism in some quarters. Young Methodism is with the President in his stand on the Licensing question. He said : "If anyone taking a gloomy view of Methodism and its prospects could be present hero and listen to what has transpired this morning be would be ashamed of himself and cast away•bis f( ars. I cannot adequately express 'my sense of the public spirit and Christian devotion which have caused sl many to come from all parts of the country and devote their Easter holidays to the considemt ion of the subjects presented here to-day. I consider the ,establi,hment of the Wesley Guild throughout the -fand.means that a now wave of great and gracious influence is going to break over the Church, and that a higher tide of spiritual life of self-denying devotion is going to revive God's work. The strength of the church in the days in which we live is in the accession to its ranks of young men and women, steeped in the highest and hest influence, first of all to be moulded by the Spirit of Christ and then to mould their generation to His will. There are some people who fear lest the Wesley Guild should introduce into the church a spirit of frivolity, and lest its members should form some- what self-centred clubs to minister to their mutual enjoyment rather than to the service of the world. Whatever may be the occasional danger—and no great movement can be carried on that is not exposed to occasional danger—the spirit which animates this Conference is the very best augury that all those evils will be overcome and that you will he able to enlist every influence and every activity in the Guild for those highest purposes for which it should be carried on—a deeper apprecia- tion of the meaning of the Gospel and a greater power to set it forth, both in word and in deed." FINANCE.

The evening session was remarkable for the. reception accorded. Dr. Lidgett. When he entered to take the chair the whole company rose and cheered for some minutes. He made an appeal to the Wesley Guild to help in-the cause of Foregn Missions. The accounts showed a decease in subscriptions of £3,000, and with an increased "expenditure the anticipated deficiency was about £6,000 to £7,000. The response was instantaneous, nearly £7OO being promised in a few minutes. Let me ask the Warrington Circuit Guilds to do their share, their representative promised £s—a very -small sum considering the numerical strength. A discussion on the " Social Responsibilities of Young Methodists "was opened by Mr. T. W. Barlow, of Accrington, and delegates were invited to submit questions. Very many were handed in and were replied to very concisely anl explicitly by the Rev. H. Carter, who is recognised as an authority on Social Service:

During the afternoon service hundreds of the

,

delegates visited the Chief Fire Station to witiles a display under the direction of Mr. F. Bayliss, Chief Olken About 600 visited the Cathedral for Evenr song, and afterwards the Dean (Bishop Weldon) anu Canons Scott and Kelly conducted them round tile building, the Dean in a brotherly address welcoming "his fellow Christians to the Cathedral." On Tuesday, Dr. J. Hope Moulton, M.A., D. Litt.' D.D., gave an address on

"THE GAINS OF BIBLICAL CRITICISM." He commended his hearers not to be afraid of earnes; criticism of the Bible, at the 'same time he 'Warne," them not to adopt off-hand and new ready-Inane' theology. Modern thought and knowledge must 10 kept pace with, but the Guilders supreme luSilless was to bring new knowledge into correlation with 016 great Book and the Man. The 'net result of criticiso, was that the Bible was purged of everything Nvhiclß was not actually in it—although popularly supPos4 to be—and secured everything that was really there' It left the Bible in the form it ought to have taken 14 first. In essential and fundamental facts it brougll us back to Christ. _ _

Mr. T. H. Pugh (of Bolton) Mr. G. W. Ridlor (Hexham-on-Tyne) and Mr. Badams (Walsall), gaCt: addresses on Recruiting and- Look-out work, seVerat useful suggestions were given and in the discussiori many. fresh ideas and 'original devices were shown to be in operation successfully in different parts of tile country.

-Wh;te- ver else may be the results of the Conference —and- they .cannot fail to be beneficial and far-reacir ing—the delegates have returned to their homes wit' a broader outlook, a wider vision and a deepening and strengthening of their loyalty and devotio4 Methodism and to their Lord and Saviour Jestle Christ •

DEATH OF MR. JOHN HATTON.

Mr. John Hatton, who was at one time a velis familiar figure in Warrington., passed away on Goo(' Friday at 34, Leinster Gardens, Runcorn, where. h", had lived in retirement for some years. The decease", gentleman, who h ad attained the ripe age of 80, ha" been failing in health for some four years, and had to relinquish the quiet walks abroad which he used enjoy so frequently. He, however, took a grE't interest in literature and spent many happy hours O, his library.. When in Warrington Mr.Hatton ocCuptea a very respected place amongst the prominent trades' men of the town. Born at Harper's Farm, Padg,ate, on April 26th, 1828, he was in 1842 apprenticed to the late Mr. Isaac Piekton, chemist, at the shop noW occupted by the National Boot Company in Horse- market-street, Warrington. The business was suhse" quently removed to Market Gate where Mr. Rattail was taken into partnership and the firm becaur known as Pickton and Hatton. Mr. Hatton was 1" business for 55 years. He was a devoted Church- man and was warmly attached to Holy Trinity -Churc. of which he was 'the first people's warden, being appointed in 1870 and continuing in office for Over 20 years. He was one of the founders of the Warring ton Working Men's Permanent Benefit Building Society, and was the last of the original trustees? 3 position he held up to the time of his death. Although .had t4evered his connection with Warrington, IP.' Hatton never failed to take an interest in its affalr4 and he possessed a rich store of reminiscences of the Warrington of bye-gone days. He leaves a widow and four children—three daughters and, one son,

TEWFIK PASHA.

An trade ltas been issued by the Sultan approving the appointment of Tewfik Pasha as Turkish Ambassador in London. Tewfik Pasha, who is an ex-Minister of Foreign Affairs, comes to London with the reputation of being a clear-sighted Statesman of great ability.

THE WINNERS OF THE ARMY CUP. THE PRINCESS OF WALES HANDS OVER THE TROPHY.

b The Army Cup was won by the 2nd Battalion of the Royal Irish Rifles at Aldershot on 'aster at Monday. The Prince and Princess of Wales with two of their children were present th of 0, e game, and at the finish the Princess of Wales handed over the trophy to the captain —c winning team.

A RESULT OF THE CHILDREN ACT.

NURSES AT THE TAVERN DOOR.

The Children At does not permit children to enter licensed premises, and, as a ict-sequence, nurses are being placed outside public-houses to look after the youngsters. 14104 tavern in the East End of London theP nurses were kept very busy throughout Easter konday, and at one period of the forenoon they were in charge of over twenty small boys ‘4 girls. The innovation, it appears, is likely to become general.

DEARER POTATOES.

Heavy American Demand on the

British Market.

Something J a sensation Ii as been creatiSd during the seok in agricultural districts by a sudden and unexpected advance in the price of potatoes, Which for several months have been a drug in almost every market in England, Ireland and Scotland. The fine weather last week took those farmers whose ploughing was of more in portance away from deliver- ing potatoes, as hundreds of acres have yet to be ploughed, and little' if any, planting has yet been done in Lincolnshire and Yorkshire.

Planting time, therefore, is one cause of potatoes beirg dearer, but the main reason is the unprecedented demand from the United States. Shipments have been made of several hundred tons weekly, chiefly from Liverpool, but last week saw fresh developments. Owing to the failure of the American potato crop the demand for British potatoes has increased, and as high as .£7 to £E3 per ton was realised in New York City for ordinary samples, whilst "best sorts" made considerably more, and on Saturday cables indicated higher values for this week. Thousands of tons of potatoes have recently been shipped to the United States from Lancashire potato growing districts, and last week over two thousand tons were cleared away from Lincolnshire and Yorkshire, some via Hull, while others found their.way to Liverpool. To the utter astonishment of merchants, large pur- chases were made last week-end from the wholesale markets in London, and so great was the demand that the King's Cross Wholesale Market was practically cleared out in two days' time, and the potatoes bagged up and sent forward to New York. It is expected that the American demand will continue at least another month. With Germany and Holland 41so purchasing large quantities of Yorkshire and Scotch potatoes, both far table and reed purposes, prices have "jumped" up considerably.

RUNCORN ACCIDENT.

FALL OF lI6R SES _ AND EXPLOSIVE-LADEN

LURRY.

A startling accident occurred on Tuesday night at Astrnoor Bridge, near Runcorn.—A two-horse lurry was taking three tons of explosives from Runcorn to the Cos per Chemical Company, Manchester Bridge, when the chain-horse started off before the carter had applied the brake, swerved, and crashed through some railings, dropping about ten feet into a field. The shaft horse and lorry followed. One horse was killed, the other being injured, The carter luckily escaped,

WARRINGTON j CHAPTERS.

Local Politics Fifty Years Ago.

An occasional glance b-qckwards is useful as showing how far we have travelled in our methods and ideas, and we propose to give our readers a glimpse of the political workings of just fifty yeafs ago. Our chief source of information is a fugitive copy of a special newspaper which gave to the town a full report of the momentous things said and done in the heated atmospheres of the Lion Hotel and Old Music Hall.

The month of April, 1859, brought the announcement of a dissolution of Parliament and there was a consequent flutter and stir among the local politicians. This is what happened. Placards announced to the electors that meetings would be held of the Conservative and Liberal electors on the evening of April 11th, the former being asked to gather in the Lion Hotel and the latter in the Music Hall. The placard calling the Lion Hotel meet- ing had been signed by Mr. John Clare, and that gentleman was elected to the chair.

He expressed himself honoured at having been the medium of calling the meeting together. He believed they had got the name appended to it of an upright but a poor fellow. There was one thing he could say of himself, that he was plain John Clare. He had seen on the walls the names of several gentlemen with tails on their names. Fifty-six years ago he could have put Corporal or Sergeant or Captain to his name, but to-night he was only plain John Clare. (Loud cheers): He had known the member (Gilbert Greenall) from his infancy. Aye, he had known five generations of his family, and it was not in the power of man to put a single stigma to their name. He hoped they would again return him to Parliament. He was a gentle- man who had done his duty, neither seeking place nor pension, but doing his duty to his Queen, country and the borough of Warrington.

A resolution expressing confidence was moved by Mr. Beamont and seconded by Mr. James Hepherd.

Mr. Greenall, who was received amid pro- longed cheering, then addressed the meeting reviewing his past record and avowing his determination to support all measures which he felt would be for the good of the nation at large. Speeches followed from Mr. Nicholson, Mr. Pickmere, Mr. Alderman White and Mr. Alderman Chrimes and the meeting voted its unlimited confidence in Mr. Greenall.

We cannot, of course, copy in full the eulogies poured upon the member, but we should be acting unkindly if we failed to give a faint example of the picturesque oratory of the chairman, Mr. John Clare. As a sample of the picturesque oratory enjoyed by the meeting we copy_ the con- cluding remarks of the Chairman. He said they had heard Mr. White, who was a true Englishman, stock, lock and barrel. (Laughter). Mr. Chrimes was of the same kidney, they ran as well as any two they had ever seen together. (Laughter). He urged them not to be caught napping. He had something to say to his opponents when he got them on the hustings. One of them alluded to him lately; he was not afraid of him if he had him in a saw pit—(laughter)— one hand did there. (Laughter). He was charged—he knew they meant him—with using the cry "The Church and King, and down with the Rump." Now that was a very good cry. He hoped it would be put on his gravestone. It was a good cry before the speaker he alluded to was born—when he carried a musket 60 years ago. "Down with the Rump" meant "Down with the enemies of Old England." (Cheers).

===== 0002601_19090417_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LIBERAL MEETING.

The Liberal electors met in the Music Hall, which was crowded to excess. Mr. Alderman Hardy occupied the chair and stated the object of the meeting. Dr. Smith moved the first resolution, " That in the opinion of this meeting it is desirable to bring forward a Reform Candi- date at the approaching election," which was seconded by Mr. Brewtnall. Resolutions for organizing a Warrington Reform Association were agreed to, after which Mr. George Crosfield proposed and Councillor Burgess seconded the nomination of Mr. Alderman Peter Rylands at the coming election.

Mr. Rylands, who was received with loud cheers, addressed the meeting at great length, but refused to stand and concluded by pro- posing Mr. George Crosfield. He could only say that if they could induce Mr. Crosfield to stand they would secure a most able representative. He, for one, should be delighted to have the opportunity of earnestly and actively throwing all the influence he could to secure his return as member for the borough. (Loud cheers.) The course he would suggest was that the electors of War rington should sign a requisition to Mr. Cros- field asking him to contest the borough. If they applied to the Liberal eleoors of the town and asked them to take this course it would give an opportunity for a most perfect and „thorough canvass of the town. He believed there were not less than 400 names upon the register who had never voted at any previous election in Warrington, and out of these 400 there were many as to whose political opinions they had no conception. He believed that a considerable number of those electors would be found to be in favour of the Liberal party, and he might venture to say that if there were sufficient support his friend would not hesitate to place his services at the disposal of the constituency. (Cheers). _ _ _

Mr. Crosfield, on rising a second time, said he was sorry that his friend should have come to the decision he had on that import- ant subject. It placed him in a very awkward position, and one that he would be glad to see his way out of if he possibly could. He had no wish to shrink from his duty, yet if he acceded to the request it would be a great trial to him. He did not seek the honour, and he felt he had not the ability to adequately discharge the duties which if elected would devolve upon him. (Cries of "Yes you have.") If as Mr. Rylands proposed a requisition was presented to him signed by a fair proportion of the electors--.say 300—it would certainly knock from under him the ground of objection he at present had. (Loud cheers.) If his friend Alderman Rylands really insisted upon his withdrawing the motion—(a Voice: "Toss up ")—if he insisted he would have no alter- native.

The proposer and seconder having agreed the motion was withdrawn and Mr. George Crosfield was formally asked to become the candidate. A SECOND LIBERAL MEETING.

On April 23rd a further meeting was con- vened to discuss the question "Shall we have a contest irrespective of the result ? " Mr. Crosfield had intimated that he felt unable to become the candidate and Mr. Peter Rylands was pressed into the honQur. The ardent

spirits pleaded for a contest as a means of developing the fighting strength of the-party and of securing unity. Others thought it was exacting too much from their friends to seek a support which all confessed at that late period would be barren of results. It was finally decided to leave the matter to the electors themselves and circulars were sent to the Liberal electors. The decision was that "under the circumstances " a con- test was not to be recommended.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NOMINATION.

The writ arrived on April 25th. It was carried by special messenger from Preston and the Town Clerk and a number of the Town Council proceeded to proclaim it at Market Gate and other public parts of the town. The writ fixed April 29th as the day of nomination and on that day a crowd of at least a thousand inhabitants gathered in front of the Market Hall. A platform had been erected at the entrance of the hall and Mr. Greenall accompanied by 60 or 70 of his friends arrived at 11 o'clock. The writ was read by the Town Clerk .and the Mayor proceeded to call upon any elector to propose any candidate whom he might think proper. 'Mr. John Clare stepped forward and after some exchange of humour with the crowd proceeded to say that he had for the fourth time the pleasure to nominate his good friend Gilbert Greenall as a fit and proper person to represent the borough. Mr. Thewlis seconded the nomination.

The Mayor called for further nominations and as none were forthcoming he proceeded to declare Mr. Greenall to be duly elected. After the cheering had subsided the Member returned his thanks for their con- fidence in restoring to him the position he had held for the last twelve years. He promised that his future course would be like his past and that he would turn neither to the right nor to the left.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR MISCELLANY.

A correction is needed in our recent reference to the original bell of Trinity Church. This bell, given by Jonathan Blackburn and cast in 1706, hangs not at the National Schools but at Padgate Church.

** * *

It may be worth while to add to the information given concerning the• present bell of Trinity Church. Since the Norman period the daily curfew has been rung in Warrington from a town's bell kept for the purpose. Of the early bells we have no knowledge but in 1647 we find Colonel John Booth giving a Curfew Bell to hang in the steeple of the Court House. When that building was demolished in 1838 the curfew was transferred to the tower of Trinity Church and continued to ring from thence at eight o'clock each evening. Colonel Booth was uncle of Sir George Booth; afterwards Lord Delambre, who was Governor of War- rington during the Civil War.- The Colonel was afterwards knighted and on his death in 1678 was buried in Chester Cathedral. The old bell was cast by John Scott, of Wigan, and rings on apparently little the worse for its two-and-a- half centuries of service.

** * *

Another interesting feature of Trinity Church which went unmentioned in our article* is the candelabrum which hangs in the centre of the church. This formerly lighted the old House of Commons and has vibrated to the eloquence of Pitt, Fox, Burke and the numberless intellectual giants of the political past Perhaps some of our readers can tell us how it came to find a resting place in Warrington.

** * *

Reverting to our sketch of St. John's Presbyterian Church we have been able by the courtesy of Mr. William Rowe to peruse a volume of "Memorials of Alexander Hay" published in 1827 as a tribute to the memory of that distinguished and saintly minister. Though not a Warringtonian by birth he became completely identified with the town and left a mark upon it which two genera- tions have not entirely effaced. This volume of some two hundred pages reveals hini as a man of deep spirituality, whose zeal wore out his frail body at the early age of 47.

** * *

The sorrow felt through the town at his death is reflected in an anonymous poem circulated at the period. We quote a few stanzas.

No Muses now, as erst they roo'd , With Barbauld, whom they greatly loved, On Mersey's banks direct the lay And homage to fair science pay Nor Pity's self in Howard moans, Responsive to the prisoner's groans. She weeps ; her maidens sad deplore Their work of death—their Hay no more ! , The poor in sorrowing train attend With streaming eye—they mourn a friend! While groups who own the power of God, Attendant on his faithful Word Brought them to more than Goshen light. From sin and guilt's Egyptian nigh. In silence stand—his graces rise, A bow expanding cross the skies ; And reft and born, dissolved in tears, Their hope in beauteous lines appears. They yet shall meet, shall hail the Throne, • And 'fore it cast a glorious crown; Shall bless the Lord in endless day For mercies thro' their Pastor—Hay.

WIRELESS MESSAGES AT SEA.-ALLAN LINE

DEVELOPMENT.

An announcement of considerable interest in connec- tion with the development of wireless telegraphy is made by the Allan Royal Mail Line. Their steamers from Liverpool, and the Grampian and Hesperian from Glasgow, are already fitted with an apparatus which allows of messages to be received and dispatched over a certain distance, but on the two turbine s'oi mere, the Victorian and the Virginian, is now being installed the Marconi long-distance apparatus. By means of this the passengers will be kept in touch with the latest doings of the world throughout the whole of the voyage, and every day the ships will receive a summary of the most important events of the day. These messages will be printed on board in the form of a newspaper to be known appropropriately as the " Allan Line Daily News "—the first daily newspaper published on a steamer engaged in the Canadian trade from this country. Apart from the obvious advantages to be derived from the daily service of news, passengers will very naturally feel that the Allan Line have materially added to their sense of safety whilst crossing the Atlantic.

MR. JOHN DRURY.

The story of my life is a somewhat chequered' one, for my lot has been cast in many and varying places. I was born on May 24th, 1824, at the village of Rounds Green, near Oldbury, in Worcestershire. My father was a shoemaker by trade and was respected in the locality as one who was ever ready to help the poor and distressed. My schooling was of a brief and very elementary descrip- tion. When but an infant I was sent to a dame, school. Times were bad and my father being delicate, my mother had to go out to work. Later I became a scholar at a boys' school at Oldbury and had to walk to and fro, a distance of a mile each day. The end of my school days came in a rather novel way. Becoming embroiled in a fight, I was locked up in the school for punishment. Naturally such confinement was not to my liking, and I was detected in the act of making my escape through a small window some Bft. from the ground. The school- master said he would try a, new method of dealing with me, so in the evening he took me to his house and gave me the alternatives of having a supper of bread and -cheese and spending the night in the cellar or of going supperless to bed. I chose the former course and, after a substantial meal, was left in &trance vile in the cellar. With boylike curiosity I began to explore the place in the darkness and, coming across a tap, promptly turned it on. It proved to be the tap of a barrel of ale, and when the schoolmaster discovered that the precious fluid was lost, he sent a note to'my parents to this effect: "You had better keep John at home. I can do no good with him until he improves his manners."

At the age of nine, therefore, I was sent to work on a pit bank, and had to help to pick the iron stone from the output. It was winter time, and the hard work soon brought me to my senses. Next I got work in an ironworks near my native village, and had to act as striker to an edged tool maker. Having followed that occupation for about three years I became an ironworker at Bromford. On reaching the age of seventeen a restless spirit come over me and I ran away and 'listed in the 35th Royal Sussex Regiment at Dudley. A detachment of about 120 of us was immedi, ately drafted to Dungarvan, a little sea port in Ireland. Our quarters were in an old castle and we spent two years there. It may interest your readers to know how I got the soldiers their first Christmas dinner. Up .to that time it had not been the custom to allow any special fare on this festive day. It was a case of boiled beef or mutton all the year round. However, on Christmas Eve I happened to be on guard when an old Irish woman came and offered to sell me a goose for the modest sum of is. I had only 10d. in my possession (in those days soldiers had to be content with 6d. a day in addition to their ration), but the woman was willing to forgo the two- pence and I became the proud possessor of the goose which was taken to my quarters. An officer, making his usual round of inspec- tion, was not slow to notice the bird. On being told that I had bought it for Christmas he summoned me to his room, and I went, half expecting a scolding. However, when I told him that I was in the habit of having something extra for Christmas dinner, his sympathies were aroused and he said that if, each soldier would contribute is. he would see that a first-rate Christmas dinner was provided. He himself advanced £5 so that the men could pay their shillings when they felt in a position to do so. We had a rare feast and the news of our Christmas festivity was soon noised abroad with the result, I believe, that the dinner became an annual one and is now looked forward to, as a matter of course, by every "Tommy Atkins." I liked life in the army and would no doubt have done well in that branch of the services, but when I came home on furlough my mother was greatly distressed at the thoughts of my leaving again. Through the influence of a yeomanry officet the leave was extended and ultimately my discharge was purchased. My army career therefore came to an end after about two years experience. I resumed my employment at the Brom- ford iron works and in a few months was married to Catherine Madley, a good, pious, Christian girl. She died nine years ago last Christmas. From Bromford I went to Earl Granville's great iron works at Hanley, but in a short time an opportunity of bettering my position presented itself, so I went to Silverdale where I remained about ten years. Then I returned to Hanley and spent some years there before I embarked on another method of trying to make my fortune. It may be worth noting that during one short interval in the period already referred to, I had to abandon ironworking. Although a strong trade unionist I was always against unnecessary strikes and my attitude in one dispute led to my being victimised for supposed sympathy with the masters. Consequently I got temporary -work at a pottery at Hanley. Whilst there a keen Parliamentary, election took place. The Tory candidate was a master potter but as his reactionary views on temperance matters did not commend themselves to me, I worked hard for his opponent, a strong Liberal,who was returned by a good majority. During the contest my employers sent for me and naturally I approached them with some trepidation, fearing that my employ- ment might be jeopardised by my pronounced attitude in the campaign. To my surprise, however, they said: We are pleased to have such a man on our premises—one who says what he means "—and promoted me to a better post which I held until I was able to return to the ironworks.

About fifty-two years ago I came under the influence of Richard Weaver, the con- verted collier, who was preaching at New- castle-under-Lyme. There was nothing of the classical speaker ,about him, but he had a great hold on the Working classes because he could talk tc) them fr9rn experience. It

was his earnest words that led to my own conversion. About the same time I resolved to become a teetotaller and have kept the pledge up to the present day. On leaving Earl Granville's ironworks at Hanley I began business as an auctioneer of crockery etc. For about ten weeks I held sales at a small town in Gloucestershire, and it was my habit to preface business with a a short lecture on trade unionism or teetotalism, which seemed to prove popular with the people. It was as an auctioneer that I came to Warrington about thir-ty-three years ago. One day I was selling goods opposite the old Liberal Club in Market-street when a gentleman came up to me and asked if I did not know him. At first I could not recall him, but he proved to be Mr. Thomas Burton, with whom I had worked at Silver- dale. He had come to Warrington for the inauguration of Messrs. Monks, Hall and. Co.'s works and he at once asked me whether I preferred auctioneering to working at the forge. Well, although I had not done badly at my new business, I was quite ready to return to my original employment and Mr. Burton was able to offer me work at Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.'s which I held for four years. At the end of that time I started in the greengrocery business and continued it for twelve years. My next work was as the "town missionary" of War- rington. On coming to this town I had identified myself with the old Dallam- lane Chapel where a grand work was being done. They used to call us" ranters but we did not care so long as progress was being' made on the right lines. On the opening of the new chapel in Buckley-street I became a trustee and have never ceased to be warmly attached to the place. The church is in a prosperous state, although we are only a handful of workingmen. When the Rev. G. S. and Mrs. Reaney were at Wycliffe, I took an active part in mission work under their leadership and used to do a great deal of speaking iu the Brick-street neighbourhood. When they left Warring- ton, I carried on the work alone and frequently addressed meetings on Corn Hill and at Bridge Foot. For some years I also had charge of a temperance mission at the Ebenezer Chapel in Buttermarket-street. I have been living in retirement for about seven years but the temperance cause is still dear to my heart and although the cause in Warrington seems to make com- paratively little progress I have great faith in the Bands of Hope which are bound to exert a good influence on coming genera- tions.

GOLF AND DIPLOMACY.

MR. CROSFIELD'S RECENT SPEECH.

The "Liverpool Daily Post and Mercury' ha e,it leading article on Mr. Crosfield's recent speech to the Cannes Golf Club, which we published in our last issue. We extract the following : Golfers find many excellencies and merits in their favourite pastime, even when they permit it to become an obsession, as do no few in these days. There are lusty young men who spend every summer evening on the links, the whole of Saturday, and probably a good part of Sunday also, often in the mere determination to fit themselves for the winning of prizes. In such a case golf becomes not a sport, but either a business or a disease. It was not to such that Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., addressed his remarks at Cannes the other day as captain of the local club them. Perhaps we should make a reservation regarding one of his observations. He was praising golf as a training for and a mollifying i ufluence on the art of diplomacy, and certainly with one dictum the prize-hunting golf player—we prefer 'to reserve the honourable name of golfer for others- will agree : "It is only on the golf links that one finds . the work of "polite deception raised to a fine art." Such a deception, with a businesslike application, is the destruction of a fine icore card in a minor com- petition, lest handing it in should result in a smaller handicap for an approaching event, in which the "money is more.' But leaving that sordid aspect of the game, Mr. Crosfield could doubtless make out an excellent case for his claim that the links would facilitate the work of the diplomatist, and that Cannes would be better than The Hague as a meeting place for inter- national conferences. It may be agreed that when the peace of nations depends so much on the personality of Ministers—for peoples seldom make war on each other, knowing better than statesmen what an infernal business it is—such a meeting-ground as the links WOuld assuredly exercise a humanising influence upon the amity or otherwise of the various Chancelleries. Should we, ever have known the Boer war if Mr. Chamberlain an Oom Paul had played a few rounds together over Hoylake or St. Andrews? We might have found ourselves in the Burn, but not in the Modder river; we might oven have invaded the Stationmaster's Garden, but there would have been no Spion Kop ; we might, also, have lost a call or two at the Briars, but there would have heen no reason for mad mafficking. It is better to play the odd than to play the fool, and a nation can seldom make war without doing that. It is an interesting speculation, and one upon -which the cartoonists of the golf periodicals may well exercise their minds and their pencils, to imagine what might have been in Europe. during the past few months if Sir Edward Grey. von Bulow, Baron Aehrenthal, and the Grand. Vizier had given us a foursome over %the Formby links, with the Kaiser as umpire, to mal4his own rules as he went along. And if his local regulations were too novel, he might be adjudged to play Johnny Ball andthis putter over the Leasowe ground! How the four Ministers would be partnered we shall not even offer to suggest, but there are " graun' possibeelitiel." Why should they not play for the disputed provinces? It would, at least, be 'a more humane and cheaper method than any yet adopted, and, of a surety, it would be infinitely saner. We feel confident that France would consent to Arnaud Massy giving Prince Eitel a stroke in a match or Alsace-Lorraine, a game that would at once wipe font the bitter spirit of La Revanche. Happily Mr. Crosfield's little , speech was delivered to quite an international assembly, every member of which must have entered into the sense of the thing, and have gone away with the determination to make their respective Cabinets addict themselves to the Royal and ancient game, in the real interests of the human race. That is merely so far as concerns the playing of a few games; but we must not forget the actual training of mind and tempera- ment, and the invaluable enrichment of one's vocabu- lary, that follow inevitably on the flight of the fascinating ball and the donee sound of the well- handled club. For, indeed, there is diplomacy in the game—match play, of course; Colonel Bogey is no diplomatist, he must have all or nothing. There is delicacy, too, and occasionally a little mercy, more particularly in the mixed foursome, which also provides opportunities for rapprochements most real and ententes mostcordial. The man who knows how to adjust his play at nice differences between a- Kite and a Colonel, a Red Dot or a Gravit4tor ' • familiar with their respective peculiarities off wood or iron ; cognisant that each hole has its peculiarity of turf and subsoil—who more fitted than he to serve his country whether on the greensward or against the Yellow Press?

I tried to .follow you out into the world, Monsieur Henry, but I lost you there—and I shall never find you again.' "Mimi," said I, "there has always been this between vs. You call me rich, but in a few months' time I may be poorer even than these people who employ you. Is not that enough for you? Shall I make myself poor to-morrow because riches keep you from me. Is that your wish, Mimi?" "I know the truth," she said quietly; "your great Irlandais told it to me. You are rich, and if you work you will remain rich. Do not believe those who tell you that the poor are happy, Monsieur Henry. That is what the rich say to defend themselves. Oh, do you think that I can be happy so far away from France and among these strange people? Would I not return to-morrow if I could do so?"

_ "You shall return, Mimi—we will go to- gether." She laughed drolly, turning the pathos of it with the cleverness a born actress alone could command. "To the Butte," she cried, "on the high- road? All this fine philosophy of hers is no sounder than the bloom upon a flower. CHAPTER XV. (Continued.)

("The Chimes" at Yarmouth, and what Henry Gastonard learned of them.] It was a quaint scene, Paddy. Some fifty square yards were fenced in by a wooden awn- ing and provided with benches and garden. seats. The platform at the further end had a tern Union Jack for its emblem, and a Spanish flag to cross it. Here stood a piano, and two or three chairs for the performers, of whom there appeared to be five, including the lady accompanist. All were dressed in quasi- Pierrot costumes of black and yellow; and Mimi, I observed, wore precisely similar garments to the men. . As to the entertainment, it consisted of a part-song sung in three keys and wofully dis- cordant ; but Mimi left the group at the end of it, And returned shortly afterwards- in a black spangled dress and a quiet, respect- able mantilla.

I took my sent in a garden-chair to the left of the stage, and watched the child closely. She had not seen me, and I per- ceived that her whole soul went out to this business of the dancing. Perhaps it stood to her for a reflex of Paris in a dismal land. I can imagine her recalling the balls of the Butte—all the familiar faces of the Quat-Z- Arts—and believing for the time being that the music was made by Jean Delmas, and that Chardibert was her partner in the dance. She had no talent for such a class of entertainment—so much I confess at once. Her dancing was spirited, but inelegant; she threw herself about with the uncouth gestures of a child at play. When she sang, it was not in Spanish, but. in that argot of the atelier, which even a Frenchman in the audience might not have understood. But I understood it, Paddy ; and, as though she were speaking to me alone of all the com- pany, I watched her intently until her eye caught mine, and she ceased to dance as suddenly as though a strong man held her ankles to the floor. This was for the merest fraction of minute. A seedy individual in a long grey overcoat—or rather, on overcoat which Fad once once been grey—strode forward from his nook behind the Piano and addressed her in rough tones. She answered him with a nod, and continued to dance immediately, which provoked a whole torrent of bad langnage. and a subsequent draught from a substaatial flask when the back of the piano some vhat hid the man from the audience. Mimi, upon her part, now danced on as though she had never seen me at all. I did not catch her eye again, not once, until the entertainment was done and the people had quitted tho en- closare. CHAPTER XVII. [Paddy O'Connell lays down the law.] Glendalough, August 121 h, 1903. Dear Harry,—What I wou:d have you to do is to set about getting your living. 'Tis honest advice and the best I can give you— though it's precious little I do in that line myself, and ao poor hand I would be at the employment if my father—God rest his soul —had not done the business for me. .

I am little acquainted with the art of money-making, and no wise adviser in that matter. But I see plainly enough that if you do not sot about earning your living, this parson man will be banking your fortune and thanking God for it, whi:e you will be next door to a beggar in the streets, and a mighty unsuccessful one at that. Let me ask you what you could bring, to the child if you lost your fortune. This gipsy notion is well enough when a man fol- lows it by choice ' • but there is a time Of life when you begin to sniff at the cooking-pot and _to ask how many thorns are in your bed befA you and you will .come to that be- fore thost of us, by reason of the 'habits you have acquired. Indeed, Harry, lam not sure- that you understand at all what this lose of fortune may mean . to you, nor do I be- lieve your life will be worth living when. fon have lost it. You are young, you have a fine presence, people take a liking,to the looks of you, you arc by no means wanting in brains. Should you get on the Stock Exchange you would find clients enough—or, for that matter, you might turn your Art knowledge to some ad- vantage and see what you can do in that line.

Shake your wits together, my boy, and make a start, to-morrow if you. can. 1 couldn't do it myself—unless I were taken up by those who would learn to ride, or golf, or play a decent hand at bridge; but you can do it, Harry, for you have the brains; and if you love this little girl out of France, and if your heart is full because of her, you will lose no time in following my advice arta pre- paring that home she will be willing enough to occupy. Meanwhile, see, for God's sake, that no harm overtakes her. It's worse than wicked to hear of the company she is in. Pay for a change cf employment and do not let her know that you have done it. Your purse may help to achieve this. Pull the strings of it wide open, Harry, awl put her in some de- cent way of life, in London for preference, and where you can watch over her. I have said from the first that the blood of the Bo- hemian runs strong in her veins. I doubt that you will ever tie her to any house or country—but the effort is worth the making, and you know that you have my good wishes in the matter.

As for your prudence, your right to marry her or the wisdom of it, you know that I am a man who said be hanged to convention many years ago. • What a sorry peep-show, what a play of shams and meanness and false pleasures is this social world we know, and into which we were born ! I tell you that the hut on the mountain side —if there be good whisky therein and a decent golf course within riding distance is all the palace I will ever require; while, as for my friend Harry, if the great high road and the cafe at the far end of it are not his goal, then say that I know nothing of men and less of the sex that does us all the mischief.

You will set about earning your living, Harry, and put Mimi to some decent em- ployment unbeknown to her that you have done it. This is the wisdom and the wish of your old friend, PADDY.

His coloor alternated between that ,of Hr.!' departing ra-nbow and the newly-im- ported orange. There were moments when he • wa43 at a loss how to address me at all.

"Did you say that you wore venturing your own capital in these affairs?" he. asked me. The simple fellow, a child would have Seen through the question. "Not a penny' if it," said I, Ening my glaFs with the Afarna"o, old in bottle, which he buys from a neighbouring- grocer for our-and- three; "not a penny of it, Arthur. You must understand that I am bring:ng the new French motor-cab companies io London, and when the Syndicate has put down two hundred and fifty thousand, there should be sonic seven or eight for me as negottatory vendor to begin with ; and if they don't pay me twelve or fifteen hundred a year after- ward's to look after their interests, • I'm a Dutchman. What I wanted to ask you was just this : Do you think I am wise to take up such a thing as a motor-cab, or would you ad- vise me to stick to the flotafon of the new submarine company, in which you know that I have interest? You have great sigaeity and ir.-_reption, and so I put the question t,, you frankly." Upon my word, Paddy, the mitn'i face was a study when I said thin, and it was worth snything to hear him humming and hawing in the very best pulpit ma:Ater before he annwercd me.

"A elvrgyman knows little of financial affairs," he remarked, coughing slightly to cover his difficulty. " Undoubtedly, a cab ii net a very dignified conveyance, and cr— hem, the future of the motor-car is—that is --may be-- a dusty one. I should conaider the whole question very closely, Henry. There are two sides to it, as to every ques- tion."

"And to every cab, Arthur. Well, I shall take yotir advice, for, of course, if I don't do somethiffg very soon, you will be having my little lot and driving a four-in-hand to Hur- lingham. I don't -mean to let you do that, Arthur. I shall stick to the money; though, if ever you want fifty for parochial us-:s, don't forget to tell me." He was visibly upset—he is not a man who can bide -h!s feelings. I believe that if I had said the word, he would have consented to a compromise on the spot. But I am an ot-sti- nate fool, Paddy, and I feel that I vicUld sooner sleep on the Embankment without a shirt. to my back than part with one shilling to this disciple of the Law and the Profit 3 who would spend it So ill.

A huge conflagration has occurred on the mot ntains overloeking the vale of Llangolltn. At noon on Saturday the heather on the Berswn range ignited and the Ore extended over two miles. The flames consumed every pat tick of vegetation. PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica.

SUPPLEMENTARY LENSES.

For the amateur who has a long purse a collection of lenses of vat ions foci is an easy matter, but to many the possession of more than one lens is a great 'usury. It frequently happens that in photo- graphing say, an interior of a church, that your lens, which is quite suitable for outdoor work and ordinary portraiture is of too narrow an angle to yield a atisfactory result., On the other hand it may happen =ll t you are photographing out of doors where there is only one position of photographing a house, castle or similar subject, and yet the picture is much smaller on your plate than you wish it to be. The oril way to overcome this difficulty is by using a lens of a longrr foci. In such cases as these supplementary lenses, such as the Planascope lenses sra cf great service. These consist of cemented, achromatic combinations with a very neat spring attachment for fixing on to the front of a lens. They are made in several forms ; the wide angle, which reduces the focus, the telephoto which increases the focus, and also two other forms especially for copying and Portrait work The cost of these supplementary eases is only a matter of a few shillings, which is ery different co:npared with the cost of an extra lensv

south chancel window, the light from this windol°- produces a very nice effect on the altar. Those of you who only possess one lens with your camera will n" doubt be disappointed with the small amount of the church interior that you will get-on your plate. • The only way to overcome this difficulty is either to use one of the supplementary wide angle lens attachments which can be purchased for a few shillings, or else t° obtain a shorter focus lens than the one you are in the habit of using, Which necessarily gives you a wider angle of view. If you possess two cameras, say one a plate :19d the other a plate with an anastigmat lens on eacO, try using the 1 plate lens on the plate camera f"r, this work, and by stooping down to, say, Flt; you wilt find the lens (if it is a fairly good one); will fully cover the ;- plats, and, at the same time, will embrace a much wider angle of view than the half plate lens. Although this lens will cover a half plate at Fl 6, to get al4olute sharpness of seats that may be near the camera in the foreground you may find it necessary to stop down considerably more, even as 'much so F6l. This, of course, means a lengthy exposure, but long exposures sometimes have their advantages. .01 cases where visitors arc about inspecting the chureht, unless they are in very light garments, it matters bat little if they move about in front of the camera during a long exposure, whereas, in the case of a short exposure, considerable inconvenience may be caused by people viewing the church. In cases where the east window allows the passing- of a large amount of light trouble frequently arises feom either halation, or worse still, reversal in development. The trouble of halation can be remedied by haviog the plate backed, but reversal -during developme° through prolonged exposure is a difficult thing to cote with. During development if .you suspect the latter trouble it is well to have a small sable paint brush in a measure containing bromide of potassium, or sloe in a.solution of citric of soda, and if you find this window flashes up very quickly before any of thie surrounding parts appear, pour off your developer all paint over the window with this - restrainer. To ti° this well a certain amount .of practice is necessaryi otherwise you will find the restrainer will spread over the edges of the window and cause unusual markinhl in the negative. This is the one point to ho careflo about, that the restrainer does not spread. It is e' good thing to have another brush that is fairly free from water to wipe away the restrainer from any part of the negative where it runs over. Now pour on the developer over the entire plate and proceed. By these means you can keep - the .development of the wilidoo back, and in ninny cases prevent reversal of the image. Be careful in photographing these interiors that vow; camera is absolutely upright, otherwise you will fimi that the uprights in your picture will be tilting over. Lack of attention to such a little detail froquentlY 'mars what might otherwise be a successful photograPh' NEW IDEAS IN PHOTOGRAPHIC EXHIBITIONS'
