MOUNTING HINTS,

Undoubtedly for those who can afford it there is no better method of nnunting than what is known as the dry mounting method, but the cost of an outfit is rather more than the average worke wishes to give. The method can I e adopted by improvising form, use the ordinary hout•ehold flat iron instead of the press The adhesive tissues can be bought at most dealers. Tl.e great advantage of this dry mounting is that is that you avoid all trouble of cockling that frequently occurs when tther melhods are Used. If you are using sta,ch or any of the ready made mountants that are on the mar Let, and you are not mounting on paper or thin mounts, it is best to soak all the prints first cf all in water, then taking them out putting them faro downward on a glass plat-, not in a regular pack but in a heap, So that one overlaps the other. Squeeze as much of he water off the prints by hand pressure, or cover over with a cloth or blotting paper, so as to gct rid of all super linens water. You then commence to and by having the prints wet you will find that the edges will not come away from the mount, which frequently happens if the print is dry when pasting. Trouble sometimes arises if ycu have carefully trimmed your print before so as to fit a special mount by one way of the paper stretching more than another, and you will -probably find, especially if Jou are using a large size, that it has stretched out of all proportions for the mount that you have trimmed it for. The difficulty now is how to cut the strip off your print while it is still wet. It is not generally known that this can easily be accomplished by laying the print face upwards on a piece of card- -board. Put your cutting shape over this, then with a sharp penknife the strip can be cut off without any fear of jagging the edges, which would certainly cceur if you were cutting it on glass. It sometimes happens that you wish to dry a mounted pint quickly. This is very simple if you are printing on a collodion paper. In the case Of a gelatine paper it is always risky to put the print in front of the fire in ease the film n ight melt. However, a gelatine 'print may be fairly safely dried by the following method. Obtain a filly thick.sheet a cardboard, and fix this some eighteen inches or so above a gas ring. The wet rrnunted print can now be played face upwards on this card, and in the course of ten minutes you will find it dry without any trouble.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
CLEAN LENSES

I nctice the above title attached to a paragraph with two poetical illustrations in the March number of "The Photogiaphic Monthly." It shows the repro- dulion of two prints, in which one is a good sharp nepative of a house, while, %ha other one has quite a blurred and fuzzy appearance. They illustrate ‘N hat will happen if you use a lens that is covered with dust, or, as is more likely to be the case, if you have condensation of moisture on your lens. In these days when caps to lenses are so rarely used owing to the use of self capping and other shutters, this is a difficulty which may often arise without being swore cf the fact. I remember some years ago, one very cold Easter, I was taking some photographs in the early morning of some volunteer rnatucuvres and I was surpiised on development to find that the first three or four pictmes were all fuzzy, and it was a long titne before I found out the cause. It being a very cold mot ning in taking my camera, which had been through the night in a warm room, moisture immediately cond-ensed on the lens, Litt after a certain chile this cleared of its own accord, as soon as the glass chillcd down to the, same temperature as the atmosphere, which acccunted for the pictures taken ahem', aids being all right, while the early exposures were so bad.

PHOTOGRAPHING CHURCH INTERIORS.

At this time of the year the best opportunity occurs for photographing the interior of churches, as at the Easter festival the-altar frontals and chancel hangings are, in churches that use such hangings, of a white character. This may seem a small point but it is a great advantage, especially in those churches that have a large amount of light coming in through the east window, thereby lessening the \severe contrast that would otherwise occur. Stretton.

VESTRY MEETING.—The annual vestry meeting was held in the schoolroom on Monday evening, the Vicar the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross presididg. A fair number of the parishioners attended. The churchwardens pre- sented their accounts; the general account showing a small balance in hand, and the choir fund £6 12e. 6d. The following officers were elected.:—Mr. 0. W. Willett, vicar's warden; G. Blackshaw, people's warden; sides.men, Mr. Isaac Broady and Mr. Herbert Brockle- hurst ; lay representatives, Mr. Chorley and Mr. 0. W. Willett.—A hearty vote of thanks was passed to the church officials, organist, choir, sexton and others for their past services.—The Vicar alluded to the great loss the church and schools had suffered by the death of Mr. C. T. Whitley. A vote of condolence was passed in silence to the family in their sad bereavernent.—The Easter offerings which amounted to £lO 12s. 6d. were presented to the Vicar.---A hearty vote of thanks having been accorded to the Vicar for presiding, con- cluded a very agreeable meeting.

Penketh and Sankey.

PENKETH TANNERY PRIZE BRASS BAND.—Great cre.dit is due to the local band for the winning of two prizes this Easter. They journeyed to Westboughton on Saturday to take part in a contest which was held on the gala field there. There were 21 bands- competing, and the Penketh bind was awarded second prize, Stacksteads being first. Mr. W. Adamson acted as judge. On Monday the Ravenhead brass band contest found the Penketh band in grand form. 'the judge, Mr. T. Allsop, awarded them first pr:ze. J. A. Greenwood conducted in bath contests. The contest piece each day was "Andromeda" quadrilh s (W.R.) HEATH ROAD BOWLING GREEN.—This club opened the season on Easter Monday .when the members turned up in good numbers and enjoyed the popular genre. Notwithstanding the heavy rainfall in the fore- noon the condition of the green proved. satisfactory. About five o'clock the company sat down to a knife and fork tea which was ably served ps the Pavilion by Miss Booth and other lady friends under the super- vision of the Secretary, Mr. E. Diggory. Ike tea was 'rienerasly provided by the worthy president, Mr. James Dodds. At the conclusion of the repast votes of thanks were given to the President for his able assistance and donations to the club, and also to the Secretary for his untiring services. The members' competition mains commence shortly, and, given fine weather, some good games are anticipated. The green is nolvin splendid order and should afford the trundlers great pleasure. ST. Lymm.

A SMART CAPTURE.—Three Irish labourers, named Batty Killorne, Harry Hunt and James Flynn, employed at Warburton' appeared before Messrs. A. Watkin and S. W. Gould at the Lymm Police Court on Monday to answer the charge of stealing five ducks find a drake of the value of 305., the property of Edward Clarke, Moss Brown Farm Warburton.—P.O. 'ditch stated that on receiving information of the robbery on the morning of the 12th inst. be went to prosecutor's farm and made an examination of the premises. He trated blood from the fowl cote to the door of the Irishm-in's " thanty," the door of which was locked. He was convinced that whoever had committed the robbery bad been in the shanty after doing so. He get Mr. Clarke to force the door open. and cn looking under the bed found the dead ducks now produced. Witness continued his inquiries and satidied himself that the Irishmen had committed the theft. The prisoners weae consequently arrested.—Killorne and Hunt were sent to gaol for six weeks with hard labour, and Flynn for a month.—At the conclusion of the case, the prosecutor said he wished to compliment P.C. Hilditch on the smartness he had displayed in capturing the prisoners.—The justices concurred and said they thought the officer had done well.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDDING OF MR. J. L. STELFOX

The marriage was solemnized at St. Edithas' Church, Tamworth, on Wednesday, of . Mr. John L. Stelfox, of Walden Court, Newent, Gloucester, son of the late Mr. Henry T. Stelfox, of Lymm, and Miss Marion Gladys Lamb, daughter of Mr. J. Lamb, of Tamworth, and great niece of the late -General Sir John McLean, Bart., London. The bridegroom, who practises as an accountant in Oxford-streets Manchester, is well known in Lancashire and Cheshire, and particularly at Lymm, where he was at one time lion. secretary to the local Liberal Association, and took an active part in mission work in connection with the Lymm Parish Church. The bride has gained general esteem as an assistant schoolmistress at the Kettlebrook Council School. Great interest was taken in the wedding, and as the weather was all that could be desired, a large congregation assembled. The guests included friends and relatives of the bride and bridegroom who had come long distances fromvarious parts of the country. The Rev. Courtenay Clarke officiated. Mr. Rose was at the organ and played choice selections including " Nuptial March " (Guilinant). "Bridal March from " Lobengrin " (Wagner), and " Wedding March " (illendelsaohn). The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a Directoire gown of cream silk crepe de chine over silk, with panel:of gold lace • embroidery, and wore a 'Honiton lace veil over a spray of real orange blossom and white heather. Her ornament was a peridot and pearl necklet, the gift of the bride- groom-, and she carried a shower bouquet of orchids, white carnations, lillies of the valley and white roses. The bridesmaids were the Misses Lilian, Ivy, Vera,and Gwendoline Lainb,sisters of the bride,and Miss Denny Stelfox, niece of the bridegroom. The costume of Miss Lamb, who attended her sister as chief,- was .a gown of pale mauve silk trimmed amethyst, with flower hat of lilac. The four other bridesmaids were attired in Empire gowns of white muslin, with mauve sashes and Leghorn hats with ostrich 'feathers and all carried bouquets of_orchids, white carnations, lilies of the valley, and white roses. The two younger brides- maids carried lovely baskets of flowers besides shower bouquet similar to the bride's. Miss Lamb wore a handsome gold curb bra cele t,and four other bridesmaids pearl pendants,the gifts of the bridegroom. The best man was Mr. Michael Wilson Frowde, of Malvern. After the ceremony a reception was held, and the guests enter- tained at Cliffe Villa. The happy pair left for Lon- don, en route for Paris, Switzerland and Italy. The bride's travelling dress consisted of Directoire cos- twine of navy blue Sedan clath, trimmed with black silk braid, and black Gainsborough. hat, with ostrich plumes. The list of presents which were most numerous and costly, included a lovely silver fitted dressing bag, the gift of the bridegroom to the bride. The children at Kettleworth school are to be enter- tained by the bridegroom next Thusrday. . Stockton Heath. PARISH COUNCIL AIEETING.

Mr. T. R. Westbrook said that the newspaper selling on a Saturday night was becoming a perfect pest. The noise the boys made from 6.30 .to 8 o'clock wa3 awful, and something ought to be done to stop it. Mr. Hallows: Isn't it an oftence. 'I he Council were not aware that it was.

It was decided that the Clerk see the sergeant of police about the matter.

SWING BRIDGE.

Mr. Brown sAid the footpaths of the sling bridge were in an awful coLdition when it was wet. There had been a suggestion that the boards should be sloped for the a tiler to run off. That could be done with very little trouble. _ _ . _

The Clerk observed that Mr. Waring had said he would give it his attention. Mr. Brown: Have the County Council no authority to deal with the matter.

The Clerk: I think so, and be added that a resolu- tion bad been paszed in December 1908 to the effect that something should be done towards obviating the lodging of the water. Mr. Brown moved that the following resolution be sent to the Manchester Ship Canal Co.:"Whileadmitting an improvement to some extent in the conditkn of the footpaths of the Swing Bridge the Council still thinks its condition in wet weather is not as it should be, and that same further improvement shoutd be made to obviate the lodging of the water on the footpaths." This was agreed to.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

MOTHER-IN-LAW'S LOSSES.

At Widnes, on Monday, before the Mayor (Mr. T. Smith), a middle-aged man named Charles Maitland, Cholmondeley-street, was charged with stealing £3, the property of his mother-in-law. From the evidence, it appears that Mrs. Johnson placed the money in a chest of drawers, and her daughter (Mrs. Maitland paid daily visits to see tbat the money was safe. On April sth the money was missed and the prisoner left bore. He was arrested on West Bank Duck on Sunday. Only 23. gas recovered. He said he bad spent the money in Warrington and Manchester. A remand was granted.

WESTON AFFAIRS.

The minutes of the Weston Parecbial Committee stated that the Clerk reported that he had received an intimation from the Manchester Ship Canal Company that, provided the Council did require the canal bridge in Post Office-lane widened to the utmost limit of the bridge the ComPany would be prepared to assist both in the work of widening and the future maintenunce of the bridges and fences. It was also reported that the Weaver Navigation Trustees contemplated certain alterations at the bottom end of Post Office-lane which might materially affect the Council's proposals in eencection with the road. It was resolved that the elerit endeavour to asc(-rtain whether the Weaver Navigation Trustees have any, and if so, what alterations are in cratemplation, and that he report the reply at the next, meeting. The silt-committee appointed at the last meeting repel ted that they were not yet prepared with their recommendations for sewering Sandy-lane. That they were of opinion that the portion of Post Office-lane which lies on the East of South-road, passage leading therefrom in a northerly direction and the passage leading out of the last mentioned passage in an easterly direction, should all be made under the provisicns of the Private Streets Works Act, 1892. The minutes were confirmed. • _ INFECTIOUS DISEASES.

Chadwick, C.• L. Linaker, P. Ball, C. L. Linaker jun., P. Collins, W. M. Morris, .1. M;vnekyrne,.H. M. Thlbot, G. Gorton War-. button, G. Pilling, T. Guest. J. it. Davies, T. Wood. .1, Halton, J. LAND VALUES.

The Clerk read tire— following resolution which the Poplar Board of Guardiars had forwarded with a requeit to the Board to pass a similar rosoltitien " that this Board calls ths attet,tion of the Government to the alarming find continual rise in the rates of Poplar especially those connected with the educa- tional services coupled with an increasing demand for adequate provision for the sick, the aged, and infirm, fatherless and other children for this district who exceed SO per cent. of the total recipients for relief and urgently requests that provision may bo made in the fot thcoming budget for a substantial tax on land value,, both occupied and unoccupied, such tax to be used for the purpose of lightening the burden of the rates by providing out of the national funds for the necessary provision for the needs of aged and infirm and further requests that measures be also taken to secure the valuation and taxation of land 'values in such a maximum that the great landlords of this aid other districts be compelled to bear tbeir share of the cost of those public improvements which now so largely and continually increase their needs." Mr. Linaker said he was not quite in favour with the resolutions considering the nations increasing expendi- ture. He ihoeght people should le a little more care- folio the spending of public money. Regarding the taxing of land values Mr. Linaker raid the Government would be very stupid if they pawed it as it would only greatly increase the number of unemployed. It was unanimously agreed that the letter lie on the table.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ST. JOHN'S VESTRY MEETING.

The Easter vestry meeting at St. John's Church, Earlestown, was held on Monday, the Vicar (the Rev. T. R. Matthews) presiding.—Dr. Mouncey read the accounts for the past year. He said the period under review was started with a credit balance of £32 9s. 2d. The offertories realized £95, the special collections £75, for the new screen £26, donations £24 10s. (of which the sum of £2O was from Messrs. Greenall, W hitley and Co.). The Mis.sien Hall raised £l2B is. Gd. There was a balance in hand of £3l2s. Bd. The cnracy fund showed a balance in hand of £6 is. 19d. Dr. Mouncey said they had a very small balance at the bank—£3 12s. Bd. It was necessary that some effort should be made to raise money to carry on the work. They had a large amount to meet on the Ist May and there were a few outstand- ing bills, it. hid been suggested that the foothill dub should give them the grrte money at a match, or a donation, and that a cricket match should be arranged. Then by the early autumn they would le obliged to have a sale of work, as they could not go on without it.. —On the motion of Mr. Boardman, seconded by Mr. T. E. Greening, the accounts were passed.—Mr. T. Molyneux moved that Mr. George Dale be people's warden.—The motion 'was agreed to. The people's sidesnaen were nominated as follows: Messrs. J. Itylance, jun., W. Welsh, T. E. Greening, Hemmings, Richards, and Porter.—The Vioar again appointed Dr. Mouncey as his warden and named the following as sidesmen : Messrs. J. Boardman, C. J. Dean, T. Molyneux, T. Hardman Slinn, Taylor and T. A. Pimblett.—Mr. T. E. Greening moved, and Mr. T. Hardnaan seconded, that a voluntary rate of 2d. in the £ be levied, and this was agreed to, though Dr. Mouncey commented on the uselessness of it, as they get nothing , from it.—Dr. Mouncey and Mr. T. Molyneux were I appointed lay representatives.—The Vicar thanked the nardens, sidesmen, choirs, and organist., rte., at the Church and Mission Hall, who had fulfilled their duties faithfully and well.—Thrinks were also tendered to Messrs. Boardman and Dem for clerical work.

MR. SEDDON AND SHOP ASSISTANTS' GRIEVANCES.

At the annual conference of the Shop Assistants' Union at Birmingham on Mond ty, the delegates con- sidered in camera for over two hours the question of the prospective shop hours legislation. The following resolution arrived at by the Conference was afterwArds communicated :—"--That this Conference reaffirms its previous cpinion that the promised legislation for the regulation of shop hours should include the policy of the limitation of hours of all assistants to sixty hours per week, inclusive of meal times of one and a half hours per day ; no day in the week to exceed twelve hours together, with the principle of the compulsory clos7ng of all shops at stated hours ; ar.d that the principle of local option be acknowledged as the desir- able feature."

Air. J. A. Seddon, M.P., said he thought it was not egotistic to claim that they had been able to force the hand ,of the. Government and they mked them in the resolution to adhere to the sacred uromise that was made to carry out the intentions of that organisation. They had a just claim upon Parliaa ent in the present session, notwithstanding the convsted state of public business and the scares and alarms that were agitating the public mind. The granting of the moderate demand they were making would not jeopardise the convenience of the public. The public, the traders, and the assistants would all be benefited by the Govern- ment giving expression in an Act of Parliament to the promise they made a year ago. He hoped the delegates would make it their business to at once present to their Parliamentary representatives the ultimatum from the Union, and if their members of Parliament were not prepared to assist in this moderate denand the mem- bers of the Union were prepared to put in motion the machinery at their disposal. (Applause). Mr. Maclean (London) moved a resolution declaring that no legislation on the livincib•in system will be satisfactory to shop workers whieh does not propose the abolition of a demoraising cloister system which deprives adults of the rights of home, msnhood, and citizenship, and calling upon the Government to introdnce legislation based on the minority report. This was seconded on behalf of the Toxteth Baanch.

Emphasis was laid on the moral contamination involved in the system and on the overcrowding and unhealthy surroundings. Seirne speakers stated that unsound food was some- times provided, and it was a practice of some employers tomb their assistants of proper meal times. The resolution was unanimously carried. On a resolution by Mr. 'Williams (Pontepridd) that the union, having takeb over the responsibility of Mr. Seddon's parliamentary candidature, the whole of his salary shoeld be paid through the Central Office. Mr. Dermal] said he had received an intimation from the Labour party that they paid is salary of 2200 to etch Member of Parliament v%ho endorsed tle-ir pro- gramme and became a member of their efoup, and there as no probability of their assenting to Any indirect payment. The resolution fell through. Mr. Graft .n (Chesterfield) submitted a resoled-ion protesting against any form of compulsory enlistment ,for the Territorial Forces, and strongly condemning the action of firms and companies who make such enlist- ment a condition of obtaining employment. Mr. Seddon, M.P., asked delegates to apprise him of any endeavour en the part of employers to take any unfair advantage in this matter. He was convinced, he said, that employers wauld think twice before they pur- sued a dastardly policy of trying to beet military honours at the expense of the shop assistant. , The resolution was carried, and the remainder of the business vies chiefly of internal interest.

Reckon it up

How much does washing day cost you? Reckon ,it up : washerwoman, her meals, your meals, coal, soap, soda, powder, wear and tear on the clothes. It's a pretty long bill.

Now reckon up the Fels-Naptha way: Fels-Naptha and cold water. That's all. Don't even reckon your own meals, because if you start washing after breakfast you will be finished before dinner.

This is the way : Soap the clothes, roll them up singly, lay them in the water, soak half an hour, rub lightly, rinse well, and they are ready for the line. If you are satisfied, it will have cost you a few coppers. If you are not, your grocer will give you your money back. So there!

Silks

Use warm or cold water ; not hot. Soak and rinse; don't Fels- Naptna rub or wring. Iron when quite dry under a damp cloth. That's The soap with a Way of its own. all! U.%

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. - N.S.S. should only be written on one side of the paper. Letters will stand a much better chance of insertion if written briefly and to the point.

Mr. Howes and £5

Sir,—ln your issue of Saturday last you gave a cheap advertisement to Mr. Howes being in East Denbigh. Now, Sir, I also was in East Denbigh, and at a meeting used a quotation which- Mr. Howes said was a "lie," but he never attempted to prove it and in his usual bluff offered £5. This offer I accepted and promised to spend it in giving £1 each to five families who were in need. lam still waiting for Mr. Howes to prove the quotation to be a " lie " or hand over the £5 as it will be of some use to the families. _ _

He also gave men challenge to debate Tariff Reform. This I accepted and am still prepared to debate this question with him in Warrington or anywhere else.— Yours &c.,

J. ROYLE.

76, Broughton-road, Pendleton.

Play up Warrington! Sir,—When the safety of the country was in danger in 1798, 1859, 1899, 1902 this ancient and loyal Borough of Warrington was to the fore nd well to the fore in supplying men to do their part in defence of their country and when the field army brigades were formed from the Volunteers in 1901 the War- rington battalion was one of the first battalions chosen and was one of the four battalions in the 30th Field Army Brigade to take for several years to camp for 14 days 80 per cent. of its fully enrolled strength. While in that brigade it earned a reputation at,the War Office" Second to none." The battalion is now the 4th (Territorial) Battalion, South Lancashire Regiment and is alas one of the weakest, if not the weakest in point of numbers in the West Lancashire Division. Is this worthy of Warrington? Recruits of the right sort have come in in large numbers and a remnant (all honour to them) of "The old guard" of the Ist V.B. but where are the rest? _ _

_ The employers have played their part by doing all they can to make it easy for their men to respond to their couutry's call. Why are they hanging back'? Is it because they are smarting under the reduced grants for camp which their officers regret quite as much as they do and which they trust may, ere long, be rectified? Surely now, if ever, the only way to ensure Peace is to be so strong for" Defence " that no country would lightly dare to attack us. To those of "The Old Guard" who are not now members of the 4th Battalion I would say, "Your country is again in Danger !" For your Kin.-' and Country! For the sake of your old Battalion! For the honour of Warrington: Put your own private feelings on one side! Be true to yourselvcs. Play up! Play up! and play the game! Yours faithfully, F. M. APPLETON.

Intemperance and Unemployment.

Sir,—Before closing this discussion I desire to urge upon Labor Omnia Vincit a phase of the question which has so far not.been'dealt with in this corres- pondence, and which I think our friend must have overlooked when he stated that "instead of temperance reducing unemployment it would increase it." The alcoholic consumption of working class families of five members varies according to different authorities from ss. to 6s. a week, no doubt it differs with locality and fluctuates with the-ebb and flow of trade, but taking the lower figure as safe we can roughly estimate that there is spent in intoxicants in Warrington each week £3,500 or £182,000 per annum. All will recognize that this consumption is by no means equally distributed. We have first a considerable number who take no intoxicants, prob- ably a still larger number who take these beverages in strict moderation, perhaps in the form of supper beer, whose expenditure may average two shillings a week. This requires that the remainder spend con- siderably in excess of the average ss. This is con- firmed by observation. There is Tom Jones who lives street, whose family earnings run to 355. a week, out of which 15s. and often more goes in liquor. Torn is a thirsty soul and by Thursday his 15s. is melted, and his thirst not assanged, so he borrows one or more shillings from a fellow workman at 25 per cent. interest and liquidates his debt next paytime by paying is. 3d. for every is. borrowed. I am not romancing, but speaking of what is going on in Warrington at the present :time. -Tom is a decent chap but his manhood is deteriorating, his pleasures are narrowed down to those of appetite, there is a declining consciousness of brain power and a splendid constitution is *being surely undermined. Torn has a wife and family and an apology for a home, and he is dragging all down with him. His four children are at " present assisting in presenting conundrums to the Education Medical Inspection Committee. What is to be done with Tom Jones who is spending the money in drink which ought to go to help to nourish, clothe, and cleanse these four under- fed, under-weight, and under-clothed children of an Empire on which the sun never sets, but on whose lives and homes the sun never seems to rise?

Now Tom does not stand alone, he is one, of many in Warrington, and this number goes to swell the number of which L.O.V. speaks of in the numbers from various authorities who give the-average of these who are living below- the poverty line and subsisting on food inferior to that given in the work- house. L.O.V. appears to • have for such cases but his one and only cure "the nationalization, &c.," by which Tom Jones is expected to have more money to spend in what? I have no wish to exaggerate and have not done so. I know well this is not the whole question, but I protest against its littlement by L.O.V. As long as there is so much excess, as long as so much is siitnt of the weekly earnings in a thing, which' for sustenance, ranks no higher than cabbages, withdrawing as it does money which ought to go to providing the necessaries of life ; this Ev,testion ought to receive the attention of every man even although he may have some plan of social reorganization of Society as his ultimate goal. But mg. purpose in this letter was rather to emphasize the effect of the general consumption Of drink on unemployment. Suppose that this £3500 spent weekly in Warrington were diverted from intoxicants-to the --satisfaction of many home wants. In furniture to make. homely many a bare room, in shoes to keep warm the feet of many little tots, in nourishing food to maintain the strength of bread- winners as well as bread eaters ; iu the one hundred and one things '' s that go to makrlire pleasant, children happy, and ames glad. What of the industrial effect of such a change ? If our friend while putting in time waiting for the Socialistic millennium were to visit the tradesmen in the town after such a change what a difference there would be, the trading increased 20 per cent., lines sold out, orders sent to manufacturers for replacement ; a stimulus given: to production, distri- bution, and consumption all along the line, and as a result a general call upon the present unemployed labour supply. I need not point out what is so generally known that the brewing trade is one of the lowest in the labour factor in proportion to the cost of the article produced. And after it is produced, gives an article in which there is about the least strength or sustenance of any food. I have no wish ,to try and shake our Mend's faith in his ultimate remedy, but I do want him to be fair to other ameliorative agencies. lam anxious that he, having evidently given earnest thought to these difficult problems and no doubt gained a position of leader amongst his fellows should recog- nise his responsibility and speak out in no uncertain vßty in regard to the economic effects of intemper- ance. With this I leave the subject._ •

The letters of Democrat and L.O.V. in last week's issue dealing chiefly with the far larger question which was not involved when the correspondence .began, I have neither space nor time to answer: • But this must not be interpreted as any disrespect for, or want of .appreciation of the many points they put so well. • I have to read a paper this -week at Oxford bearing on .the Subject, and I will send it to the Editor, and if he thinks the views conthined therein are of sufficient general interest, he will no doubt print it when he has room ; although to the ordinary reader I fear these economic ,discussions are dry reading. Still I have put a good deal of thought into this paper and I should value, the candid opinion of my thoughtful corres- pOndents who are evidently very, much interested in these questions. I deem such correspondence to be of great value,

_,TFt

especially so if all engaged in it are able to put aside all prejudice and be more concerned in seeking the truth than in scoring empty victories. CHARLES E. PARKER. Oxford, April 15th, 1909.

[We hope to be able to publish Mr. Parker's paper in our next issue.—ED. 'Ex." J

Not Playing the Game !

Sir,—Surely we shall need to construe afresh our old friend" Faeilis decensus Averni." Shall we now translate this, " Easy are the illusions of Protection " —and surely one may add, " Swift the descent from fact and logic to make-believe and bare-faced clap- trap?" -My object in writing is to draw attention to one or two significant enough illustrations of this. I have just seen an extract from one of the latest Tariff Reform leaflets, in which, as the " Westminster Gazette " points out, the attempt is made to use the cry for additional battleships as a means of appealing to tbe unemployed. "The Government," says this precious leaflet, " knew that there were thousands of unemployed in this country. Did they hasten or increase our poor little [Naval] Programme? Not a bit of it. . . . . -Our Navy and our unemployed _ .

may both be starved together and soon will be-if you don't turn this Government out."

What are we to say about the people who write this sort of stuff, and—worse still—of the chiefs of the Tariff Reform agitation who sanction the circulation of such statements by the million? _ _ .

There is no saddei• problem that modern civilisation has to face than that of unemployment, a malady which In single remedy can by any possibility cure. There are many different ways in which we may be able to reduce this tragic evil to a minimum; but what are we to say of this pretence to unfortunate people suffer- ing from employment? If attempts to use and to dupe people in the country who have been suffering from this terrible evil Of unemployment are to be made by the deliberate and wholesale circulation of such reck- less misstatements then indeed it looks as if in the cominc, strugle not a few people in the country are prepared to throw scruple as well as fact to the winds in their efforts to mislead the more ignorant part of the democracy. It is only fair to remember that both Mr. Balfour and Mr. Long have dissociated themselves from the more extravagant and unscrupulou§ statements on the question of unemployment which have been made, and are still being made in this wholesale fashion through the country. On the Naval question also, in the letter which I had the privilege of addressing to you a few weeks since, I pointed out there was nothing jn the tone of Mr. Balfour's opening speech in the House of Commons to which exception need under the circum- stances be taken, even by those who differed from his conclusions on the matter. But now alas we find Mr. Balfour laying himself out to please Tariff Reformers by hitching their schemes for Protection on to•the generous and patriotic offer of a battleship—and in case of need of two battleships—made by New Zea- land. And so we have him asking last week whether we are going to accept " this immense boon without giving anything in return" ? I think it is very important to note the answer to this question given very promptly by Sir Joseph Ward-, the Premier of New Zealand, who speaking last Friday, said," New Zealand had not made her offer in the interests of Preference. or for commercial reasons, but simply to strengthen the Empire upon the seas." That is an admirably straightforward statement • and Sir Joseph went on to add, what is none the less significant, namely, that he" regretted that the offer had been used for party purposes in England." A rebuke from the Colonies which I submit was thoroughly well deserved. Yours etc., Hotel Belle Vue, Cannes, April 7th, 1909.

A. H. CROSFIELD.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
The Daylight Saving Bill.

Sir,—Now that we have in our hands a draft of the Bill, "To promote the earlier use of daylight in certain months yearly and for other purposes relating thereto," we can sea how we should stand with regard to it if it should pass into law. It is proposed to enact as follows :

1. "From two o'clock in the morning Greenwich Mean Time in the case of Great Britain and Dublin Mean Time in the case of Ireland of the third Sunday in April in each year until two o'clock in the morning Greenwich Mean Time in the ease of Great Britain and Dublin Mean rime in the ease 6f behind of the third Sunday in September of each year, the local time shall be in the case of Great Britain one hour in advance of Greenwich Mean Time in the case of. Ireland one hour in advance of Dublin Mean Time. 2. "The time hereby established shall be known as Summer Season Time in Great Britain and Ireland and whenever any expression- of time occurs in any Act of Parliament, deed, or other legal instrument, the time mentioned or referred to shall, unless it is otherwise specifically stated, be held in tin case of Great Britain and Ireland to be Summer Season Time as prescribed by this Act." 3. "Greenwich Mean Time as used for the purposes of Astronomy and Navigation shall not be affected by this Act." These are the main provisions of the Bill. - Clause wouldappear to be superfluous as, it refers to a matter 'entirely beyond human control

The first thing that strikes one on reading this document is the inequality or lopsidedness of the pro- posal. Had it been applicable to the current year for: instance, the . eighteenth of April being the third Sunday of the month, Monday - the nineteenth would have been the first working day on which summer season time would come into force. At Warrington and throughout Lancashire, sunrise will occur that morning a feu- minutes past five o'clock and at the most westerly towns in the country, it will take place as late as fifteen minutes past five G. M. T. There may therefore be sufficient daylight to enable indoor workers to com- mence their labours at five o'clock without artificial light. It will not be so in September, the third Sunday of which will be the nineteenth day of the month, and Saturday the eighteenth would be the last of summer season time. But at Warrington and in Lancashire generally the sun will not rise that morning till ten Minutes to six o'clock and later still in towns further west. As there is usually not sufficient, daylight half an hour before sunrise at that season of the year for the WOrk of spinning and weaving factories, engineers, fitting shops, and kindred ekablishments to be carried on without artificial light, this would have to be resorted to in the mornings in the factories and workshops of the country wtich commence work at six o'clock, for two to three weeks before the close of summer season time, and this will be accentuated in the western counties. In other words, lighting up in the mornings would commence a month earlier than usual. Should the Bill as it is drafted become law it will thus operate to increase the consumption - of artificial illumintuits in all the factories and workshops of the country which commence work at six o'clock in the morning, or otherwise it will cause a serious loss of efficiency during the first working hour of the day for two or three weeks before the close of summer season time.

So far as available daylight is concerned the eighteenth of April corresponds with the twenty-fourth of August; and the promoters of the Bill, seem to have overlooked the fact that there is not sufficient daylight in these latitudes to enable indoor workers to com- mence operations in the morning for more than four months out of the twelve without the aid of artificial light. It shonld also be remembered_that there is practi- cally always the same amount of daylight at six in the morning as at six in the evening throughout the summer season, near the Meridian of Greenwich, and the fact that the great majority of our industrial establishments are situated a considerable distance to the West of .the longitude of Greenwieh militates against the utility of-the measure. Tampering with our time keeping in the manner proposed by the bill will have an adverse effect on the econopical working of our factories and so far as most of our industrial establishments are concerned the most appropriate title for it as it is draffed, would. be " The Daylight Wasting Bill."

It is q—uite obvious that the Bill as it stands requires ffirther and serious consideration before it is passed into law, and then the benefits conferred by the innovation mast far more than counterbalance the confusion and inconvenience likely to be caused by it, before if could be regarded as a useful piece of legislation. Whether this result would be-attained remains to be seen. Judging from present appear: ances it is, to say the least, extremely doubtful.— Yours faithfully, A FELLOW OF THE ROY %I ASTRONOMICAL SOCIETY. Warrington, 14th April, 1909: •

NATIONAL TEACHERS' UNION. The Morecambe Conference.

NEW PRESIDENT RECOMMENDS SECULAR

STATE SCHOOLS.

The fortieth annual Conference of the National Union of Teachers was opened on Monday, at More- cambe—" The Naples of the North." as the Mayor (Alderman E. Barnsbee), making the best of a good opportunity, described the town. Rain was falling heavily when the delegates assembled at ten o'clock in the morning, but the storm passed away, and the afternoon was fine and bright, with a strong breeze from the sea. Practically all the delegates, liumbering nearly 2,000, had arrived on Monday and the majority of them were present for the preliminary proceedings, which included a public reception, the installation of the new President, and the delivery of the Presidential address. The beauti- fully decorated Alhambra Palace is the meeting place, and it was crowded to the doors, the delegates occupy- ing the area, and the gallery being reserved for the general public. The retiring President (Mr. W. A- Nicholls) was in the chair until the installation of his successor. Among those on the platform were the Mayors of Morecambe and Lancaster, Sir W. E. M. Tomlinson, Mr. J. IL Yoxall, MT. (general secretary), and Mr. Ernest Gray. Dr. Macnauaara, M.P. wrote that he had been obliged to go to South Wales on Admiralty work.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
SECULAR STATE SCHOOLS. PRESIDENT'S SOLUTION OF EDUCATION SQUABBLES.

In his presidential address at the Morecambe Confer- ence of the Nationair Union of Teachers, Mr. C. W. Hole (Stoke Newington), who succeeds Mr. A. R. Pickles, of Burnley, said that the only solution of the education problem was to be found in State-controlled schools in which secular teaching alone was given. He warmly praised the new code, believing that it would tend to greater efficiency in the.scheols._ _

President after president of the N.U.T., he said, had pleaded for a simple course of Bible instruction in our schools, such as that set forth by the London Education Authority—a course which for many years had satisfied the consciences of the parents of the children attend- ing the Council schools of the Metropolis. Such a plan, however, had not received the approval of con- tending theologians, and therefore it appeared useless to urge further a scheme which was bound again to be rejected. _

biscussing the religious difficulty, he said there was truth in the contentions of both sides, but the price asked by the Church for admitted grievances was far too bfb. (Hear, hear, and No, no.") It appeared to him that there Were two courses open Ni luch were logical, and which gave equal treatment all round. The first vas—concurrent endowment of all types of elementary schools, viith full facilities for the lost:rue- tioa of the children in the faith of their parents. (Hear, hear.) This course, although just in principle, was manifestly unacceptable in practice. Secular education suggested the only last solution, therefore, since all others appeared to fail. Sacrifices were bound to be made all round, and the State must disendow all sectarian and religious teaching and concern itself only with the provision of secular education. (Cheers, and cries of dissent). _ _ _ _

Speaking for himself, and not claiming that a single member of the conference was in accord with him, he must say that he had been driven to the conclusion that in this solution lay the largest measure of practical justice and equity that could be meted out to contend- ing claims, provided always that where denominations bad furnished the funds for building their schools the "public must pay a fair rent for the occupation of the premises, and keep them in a satisfactory state of sanitation and repair. (Cheers). The changes which will be effected by the code for 1909 were 'highly desirable and necessary. It remained for the Government to provide adequate financial assistance in order that the size of the classes might be reduced and the staff rendered efficient in number and quality, to put the coping stone on the glorious strncture of a perfect educational system for the children of the nation. (Cheers.)

ON HEARING OF THE DEATH OF SWINBURNE.

Proud soul of song and aunrise, last great singer Of all the golden choir, thou, too, art gone; And, as mine eyes upon the sad words linger, - This "nest of singing birds "seems strangely lone. Out in the garden a wet wind is wailing, The building throstles suddenly grow dumb ; And song within my own poor heart is failing, And the hot words seem strangely loth to come, I fain had hoped to some day see the glory Of that bright face, those "flame-like" locks of thine; But Death, alas! has once more been before me, And darkly withered that sweet dream of mine. But through my soul the songs of thee are ringing, On sunrise plumes thy flowing form I see, As, with bowed head, I timidly come bringing This little tribute of my love to thee. Thine was the last of the immortal voices, That filled our England with triumphant song ; With golden daffodils the land rejoices, In every grove the blackbirds find a tongue. But thou art silent ! Never more to greet them Will thy great heart of music turn to fire ; The singing waves leap up the land to meet them, The Morning Star sings on, and will not tire ; But Death lays low their last immortal poet, And seas andstrelms and mountains well might weep; The welkin flames with sunrise, and, below it, A glory broadens on the boundless deep; But never more, with morning's fiery splendour, Or whoa the burning stars defy the dark, Will thy 'great harp its answering music rendsr, • The soul of the) out-sing the soar:ng lark— Ah, never more! and yet, thy mighty rassion FOr love, and freedom, and the tameless awl, Thy scorn of tyrants, kings and priests, shall fashion The unborn generations of the free. Within the Abbey walls they might have laid thee, Among the mightiest of England's dead; No lesser honour would have quite repaid thee, Who with such spleadours crowned her sacred head. And, yet, methinks, thy slum hers shall be sweeter In that gorse-lighted, sun-kissed pla.ce of graves: What requiem for thy stormy soul is sweeter Than the eternal music of the waves? What pomp could match the violets softly springing Where the bright woodlands quicken into green? What other music suit thy deathless singing? What fairer garlands wreathe thy brow serene? But England needs new singers, seldom needed, Such trumpet-throated lords of son; as now; The tide of fame has round her shores receded, - Last of the immortal company art thou ! Mother, awake ! What deadly spell enthralls thee? A palsy creeps through those bright limbs of thiee ; In vain the clarion voice of Duty calls thee, Men mingle poison with the sacred wine, Sweet luxury, like a sinuous snake, enfolds thee, . The glory of thy sea-blue eyes grows dim; The deadly .grasp of crawling Mammon holds thee, And Faction seeks to tear thee limb from limb ! Mother, awake! Within thy fertile bosom Two thousand years their streneos seed have sown; The land once more bursts brightly into blossom ; The seas still sing to thee! but thou art grown A little deaf to the Eternal Voices, At Duty's call art sometimes strangely mute; Pe chance thine ears are deadened with the noises Of clamoraus Trade, or Pleasure's foolish flute ! Awake! awake! awake ! ere Doom be written, As once amid Belshazzar's golden feast; Awake before thy palace galls be smitten, "Andi all the realm reels back into the beast ! " 0, for some loftier vaice than mine to rouse thee, Same trumpet throat like his thy soul to thrill ; To break the deadly lethargy which bows thee, And bid thee cry, " Lo, I am England still !" ARTHUR BENNETT. ,

- WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB.

The inauguration of the season took the form of a successful smoking concert held on Thursday evening in the Patten Arms Ilot?1. The chair was taken by Mr. T. P. Throssell, the captain of the club, who in the course of a few remarks reminded the members of the opening run, to take place to-day (Saturday). The run would be to Warburton, and he hoped there would be a good attendance and that fine weather would prevail, thus helping to make a gcod opening outing for the reason. An excellent programme was submitted, the artistes being—Messrs. J. Draper, Wallace Bay, Herbert Pltunpton, W. A. Lloyd, George H. Smith and G., Anderton. The function was one of the most enjo3 able on record, and augurs well for the coming season.—As above stated, the opening run will take place to-day (Saturday), when the quaint and picturesque village of Warburton will be the destination. A splendid programme has been arranged for the season, including bowling mains on the Sankey Chapel House green, which has again been exclusively reserved for the members. The.bowling season will commence on Wednesday, May sth, and the competition for the Watkins Challenge Cup and other prizes on Wednesday, May 26th. The red letter fixture. the President's and. Ladies' day, again appears, when the club will be the guests of the President at his beautiful Cheshire home. Other fixtures of speoial interest are inter-club runs with other organizations, cricket match with Warring- ton Thursday,fishing match,tours and week-end outings. The musical events will also bea great attraotion.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS.

School Managers Decline to Give

Them Notice.

A conference of the managers of all schools affected by the resolution recently passed by the Education Committee with regard to the dismissal of married female teachers was held in St. Paul's schools on Wednesday evening. The meeting had been called at the instance of the Foundation Managers' Association of Church of England Schools, and it was presided over by the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis). There was an. attendance of eleven. The proceedings were private and no official information was vouchsafed to the Press representatives at the close. We learn, however, that it was decided to write the Education Committee intimating that the managers would decline to give the teachers -notice but stating no grounds for that action. This was carried with one dissentient. An amendment was moved that three months' notice, from April 30th, be given to the married female teachers to terminate their services, .but there was no seconder and con- sequently it fell to the ground.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Love Rules the World."

The return visit of this popular domestic drama was cordially greeted by a crowded house on Monday evening, and patronage has been good throughout the week. Although conceived on orthodox lines, the play _is* sufficiently thrilling to satisfy the most sensation-loving theatre-goer, while the comic element, which is of the broad type, creates peals of laughter. Messrs. Sidney Rennef and G. M. Copping's company gives a satisfactory performance, Mr. Rennef himself presenting a very clever study of Jim Dixey. Mr. Stephen Langton as the relentless Stephen Blight is so realistic as to be the object of whole-hearted execra- tions from the " gods " and Miss Grace Jones (Flo Morton) as his accomplice displays no mean skill: Mr_ Will Heep (Elarold Kingston) and 'Miss Marie Clavering (Maud Carruthers), the long-suffering her') and heroine, soon enlist the sympathies of the audi • ence, for both play their parts Nvi th great power and pathos. Mr. Fred Elvin as Billy Binks and Miss Kitty Terries as Kitty Snowdrop are responsible for a great deal Of boisterous fun.

===== 0002601_19090417_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
PALACE AND HIPPODROME>

The Easter week attractions at the Palace and Hippodrome are really " high-:class and crowded "houses "have been the order. There is not a poor turn in the " bill" and the place rings with applause from first to last. Harry Tate's No. 1 Company in " Fishing" is making a first visit to Warrington and proves a great draw. The sketch is screamingly funny and remarkably well produced. Of course, Tate is not present in the flesh, but his substitute has ''o•ot the mannerisms of the master and in addition the sketch possesses sufficient merit in itself to be amusing. As a rule we ate not partial to musical extravaganzas, but we have nothing but praise for the Seven Bramusas in "Music Afloat." They are described as " an English family of juvenile musicians" and in age range from ten to seventeen. Their special line is a brass band extravaganza with the title mentioned above. The instrumental work of the party is excellent, and the music of a popular character. One's ears are assailed with pleasing sounds, but the greater treat is for the eyes. Not only are the performers smartly turned out, but the setting of the main,pieces—a view on the saloon deck of a steamer, and moonlight on the Mississipi—is really wonderful for its realism and beauty. Altogether the Seven Bramusas are a credit to someone's originality and cleverness. Two of the best comedians seen here for some time are Bennett and Martell, the shipwrecked mariners, whose turn is full of drollery which makes mirth infectious. St. Ruth and Conyers appear in old English costume as duettists and deserve to rank high in their particular line, for both ladies possess tuneful voices and blend their singing with complete harmony. Shrillo earns a cordial reception as a whistler of operatic airs, and Norah Desmond, American song illustrator, soon has the audience in full swing with her catching songs. The pictures illustrating the words are specially good. Walter Clyde, tenor vocalist, sings a couple of ballads in good style, and Mr. Widdop's orchestra is respon- sible for some very effective selections. The biograph again presents pictures which are new and pleasing.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME,

The first, which will be" The Silver Bax," a play in three acts by John Galsworthy, will be given on Thursday, April 22nd. Mr. Galsworthy's play proved a success when it was produced at the London Court Theatre some two years back and it possesses the added distinction of having gained the favour of New York audiences. It is a play which is a veritable triumph of simple realism and truthfulness to life. It made a great impression on its audience when it wasii.roduced a few weeks ago at the Gaiety Theatre, flanchester. One may be a regular playgoer in the very centre of theatrical activity for many years with- out seeing anything so fine as the Police, Court scene, which is the great feature of" The Silver Box."• There is great drama in this scene and it is the climax to a very absorbing story.

Seats for the' performance at the Palace may now be booked.
